On the Mixtures and Powers of Simple Drugs

De simplicium medicamentorum temperamentis ac facultatibus · Kühn XI 379-892 and XII 1-377 · books 1-11

No complete published translation into English by a scholar that we know of. An English translation of books 1-5 by J. Wilkins is announced in the Cambridge Galen Translations. Books 6-8: a French translation with the Greek by C. Manco (doctoral thesis, Montpellier and Bologna, 2020). The prefaces of books 6-11 and the first two entries of book 6: English by M. Coughlin (ancientmedicine.org, 2023). All eleven books: a free French version by H. Fayard (1548). An unreviewed machine translation into English of Kühn XI and XII, the volumes containing these books, was posted at Source Library (sourcelibrary.org) during 2026. This is rendering A, made by a language model and not yet checked by a scholar. The small grey numbers are the pages of Kühn's Greek edition in books 1-5 and the entry numbers (book.chapter.entry) in books 6-11. Passages with a red rule failed the machine checks; those checks are automated agreement tests, not a reading of the Greek, so a passage without the rule is not checked either. Words in square brackets were supplied by the model for the sense; Greek in parentheses gives the original word; any remark about a doubtful or corrupt reading is the model's, not a scholar's.

Check (b): the two renderings agree on at least 70% of the unit's target terms. Check (c): a model judge, salted with 10 planted errors and 10 anchors it had to get right, finds at least 80% of the term renderings within the LSJ entry's senses, with at most 2 outside. A unit fails when it fails two of three.

Flags, under each passage: places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors. Sentence by sentence, each rendering is compared with the Greek for numbers, negations and words of more or less, and for the names of drugs and parts of the body (a name in the English with none in the Greek, or a name in the Greek with none in the English). These are word-matching tests: a flag often comes from a sentence pair that is aligned a little off, a paraphrase or a name the model kept in Greek, and a passage with no flag is not checked by them either. The flags play no part in the unit verdicts.

Greek (Kühn)
αὗται μὲν οὖν αἱ ποιότητες, ὑγρότης τε καὶ ξηρότης, 705 θερμότης τε καὶ ψυχρότης εἰσί. τὰ δ’ ἀπ’ αὐτῶν παρονομαζόμενα σώματα τά τε κοινὰ πάντων ἐστὶ στοιχεῖα καὶ τὰ κατ’ ἐπικράτησίν τινος τούτων, ὑγρὰ καὶ ξηρὰ καὶ ψυχρὰ καὶ θερμὰ προσαγορευόμενα, καὶ τὰ πρὸς τὸ σύμμετρον ὁμογενὲς, ἢ ὁμοειδὲς παραβαλλόμενα καὶ τὰ πρὸς ὁτιοῦν τὸ ἐπιτυχόν. περὶ μὲν δὴ τῆς ἐν τούτοις διαφορᾶς εἴρηται πολλάκις, εἴρηται δ’ οὐδὲν ἧττον καὶ ὅπῃ διαφέρει τροφὴ φαρμάκου, καὶ ὡς ἡ μὲν τροφὴ κρατεῖταί τε καὶ νικᾶται πρὸς τοῦ τρεφομένου. τὸ φάρμακον δὲ ἔμπαλιν αὐτὸ νικᾷ καὶ κρατεῖ τοῦ σώματος οὗπερ ἂν ὑπάρχῃ φάρμακον· ἐν τῷ πρός τι γὰρ ἑκατέρων εἶναι τὴν νόησιν. ἐδείχθη δὲ καὶ ὡς ἤτοι κατὰ μίαν ἡντιναοῦν ποιότητα τὸ φάρμακον ἀλλοιοῦν πέφυκεν, ἤ θερμαῖνον ἢ ψῦχον ἢ ξηραῖνον ἢ ὑγραῖνον ἢ κατὰ συζυγίαν τινὰ τῶν εἰρημένων, ἢ καθ’ ὅλην αὐτοῦ τὴν οὐσίαν, ὥσπερ τῶν τε δηλητηρίων ἔνια καὶ τῶν ἀλεξητηρίων οὐκ ὀλίγα καὶ τὰ καθαίροντα πάντα καὶ τῶν ἐπισπαστικῶν ὀνομαζομένων πολλά.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀλεξητηρίων, ἐπισπαστικῶν
These, then, are the qualities: wetness and dryness, 705 heat and cold. The bodies named after them are both the elements common to all things and those which are called wet and dry and cold and hot according to the predominance of one of these, and those which are compared with the well-proportioned member of the same genus or of the same species, and those compared with anything whatever that happens to be at hand. Now concerning the difference among these it has been said many times, and no less has it also been said in what way nutriment differs from a drug, and that nutriment is mastered and overcome by the thing being nourished, whereas the drug, conversely, itself overcomes and masters the body of which it is a drug; for the conception of each of the two lies in the relative. It was shown also that a drug is naturally such as to alter either in respect of some one quality, whichever it may be, heating or cooling or drying or moistening, or in respect of some conjugation of those mentioned, or in respect of its whole substance, as do some of the deleterious drugs and not a few of the alexipharmic ones and all the purgatives and many of those called epispastic.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πρὸς τὸ σύμμετρον ὁμογενὲς, ἢ ὁμοειδὲς παραβαλλόμενα, ἐν τῷ πρός τι γὰρ ἑκατέρων εἶναι τὴν νόησιν, ἀλεξητηρίων, ἐπισπαστικῶν
These, then, are the qualities: moistness and dryness, 705 heat and coldness. The bodies named after them are, first, the elements common to all things; second, those called moist and dry and cold and hot according to the predominance of one of these qualities; third, those so called when compared with the well-balanced body of the same genus or of the same species; and fourth, those so called in comparison with anything whatever that happens to come along. Now the difference among these has been discussed many times. No less has it been said in what way nourishment differs from a drug: nourishment is mastered and overcome by that which is nourished, whereas the drug, on the contrary, itself overcomes and masters the body of which it is a drug; for the conception of each lies in the category of relation. It was also shown that a drug naturally alters either according to some one quality, whichever it may be, heating or cooling or drying or moistening, or according to some pairing of those mentioned, or according to its whole substance. Some of the deleterious drugs act in this last way, as do not a few of the protective remedies, all the purgatives, and many of those called attractive.

Greek (Kühn)
περὶ δὲ τούτων ἔτι μοι δοκῶ 706 προσθήσειν ἐν τοῖς ἑξῆς τὰ λείποντα. τὰ δὲ κατὰ μίαν ἢ δύο ποιότητας ἐνεργοῦντα περὶ τὸ σύμπαν ἡμῶν σῶμα κατὰ τόδε τὸ γράμμα δίειμι, λαβὼν κᾀνταῦθα πάλιν ὑπόθεσιν ἀποδε δειγμένην ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν, ὡς τὰ πλεῖστα τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ἀνομοιομερῆ τε καὶ σύνθετα κατά γε τὴν ἀλήθειάν ἐστιν, ὀνομάζεται δὲ ἁπλᾶ τῷ φύσει τοιαῦτα ὑπάρχειν οἷάπερ ἐστὶν, οὐδὲν ἐξ ἐπιτεχνήσεως ἡμετέρας προσειληφότα. καὶ μὲν δὴ καὶ ὡς τὰ μέν τινα παχυμερῆ καὶ γεώδη ταῖς οὐσίαις ἐστὶν, τὰ δὲ λεπτομερῆ καὶ ἀερώδη, τὰ δ’ οἷον ὑδατώδη τε καὶ μέσα τῶν εἰρημένων, ἐπιδεδειγμένα καὶ ταῦτ’ ἐν τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν λόγοις ὑποθέσεις ἔστωσάν μοι πρὸς τὰ μέλλοντα λεχθήσεσθαι. τούτων οὖν ὑποκειμένων ἀρκτέον ἤδη τοῦ λόγου.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But concerning these matters I think I shall still 706 add what is lacking in what follows. Those drugs, however, which act upon our whole body according to one or two qualities I shall go through in this book, taking here again as an assumption what has been demonstrated in the preceding books: that most of the simple drugs are, in truth at least, non-uniform in their parts and composite, but are called simple because they are by nature such as they are, having received nothing in addition from our artifice. And indeed also that some are coarse-particled and earthy in their substances, others fine-particled and airy, and others as it were watery and intermediate between those mentioned—these points too, having been demonstrated in the preceding discourses, let them stand for me as assumptions with a view to what is going to be said. These things, then, being laid down, one must now begin the discourse.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀποδε δειγμένην (Kühn's text divides the word; read as ἀποδεδειγμένην, 'demonstrated'), ἀνομοιομερῆ (rendered 'anhomoeomerous', i.e. not uniform in their parts)
Concerning these, however, I think I shall 706 add what is still lacking in what follows. Those that act upon our whole body by one or two qualities I shall go through in this book. Here again I take as a hypothesis what was demonstrated in the preceding books: most of the simple drugs are in truth anhomoeomerous and composite, but they are called simple because by nature they are such as they are, having taken on nothing further from our artificial treatment. Moreover, some are coarse-particled and earthy in their substances, others fine-particled and airy, and others as it were watery and intermediate between those mentioned; since these things too were shown in the preceding discussions, let them stand as hypotheses for me with respect to what is going to be said. These things, then, being laid down, the discussion must now be begun.

K11.706-707K. XI 706–707, ch. 5.2 difficulty low (1.13/100; thin 1, absent 1) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
Ἡ χρεία τῶν φαρμάκων τοῖς ἀνθρώποις γίγνεται πολλάκις μὲν ὡς αὐτὸ τοῦτο μόνον, ἤτοι θερμαινόντων ἢ ψυχόντων ἢ ὑγραινόντων ἢ ξηραινόντων, ἢ κατὰ συζυγίαν τινὰ τούτων ἐνεργούντων, ἐνίοτε δὲ ὡς ἤδη τὸ πέρας τοῦ μετρίου κεχαλασμένον, ἐπιτεινόντων τε καὶ συναγόντων, 707 ἢ τὸ συντεταμένον χαλώντων, ἢ ἀραιούντων τὸ πεπυκνωμένον ἢ πυκνούντων τὸ μανὸν, ἢ μαλαττόντων τὸ σκληρὸν ἢ σκληρυνόντων ἀμέτρους μαλακότητας, ἢ κενούντων τὸ πλῆρες ἢ πληρούντων τὸ κενὸν, ἤ τι τοιοῦτον ἕτερον ἐργαζομένων. ὁ μὲν γὰρ ἐψυγμένος οὐκ ἰατρὸς μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἰδιώτης ὁστισοῦν, ὑπ’ αὐτῆς τοῦ πράγματος τῆς φύσεως ἀγόμενος, ἐξευρεῖν ἐπιθυμεῖ θερμαῖνον φάρμακον, οὕτως ὑπὸ καυσώδους πυρετοῦ διακαιόμενος ἐμψῦχον. οὕτω δὲ καὶ πλαδαρὸν ἕλκος ἔχων ἰδιώτης ὁστισοῦν ξηρᾶναι κελεύει τὸν ἰατρὸν αὐτὸ, καὶ εἰ ξηρὸν εἴη καὶ ἄνικμον, ὑγρᾶναι. καὶ δὴ καὶ ξηρότητος ἅμα θερμότητι καθ’ ὅλον αἰσθανόμενοι τὸ σῶμα, ὥσπερ καὶ οἵ τε ἐγκαυθέντες καὶ κοπωθέντες λούεσθαί τε ποθοῦσι καὶ πίνουσιν ὕδατος ψυχροῦ, καὶ πᾶν ὅ τι περ ἂν ὑγραίνειν τε ἅμα καὶ ψύχειν ἱκανὸν ᾖ, τοῦτο ἐξευρίσκειν μηχανῶνται, καὶ πορίζονταί γε πολλάκις ὑπ’ αὐτῆς τῆς φύσεως ἀγόμενοι πολλοὶ τῶν ἰδιωτῶν τὰ τούτων ἰάματα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὡς ἤδη τὸ πέρας τοῦ μετρίου κεχαλασμένον
The need for drugs arises for human beings often simply as this alone, namely of drugs that either heat or cool or moisten or dry, or that act according to some conjunction of these; but sometimes as of drugs that tighten and draw together what has already been relaxed beyond the limit of the moderate, 707 or relax what is tensed, or rarefy what has been condensed or condense what is loose, or soften what is hard or harden immoderate softnesses, or empty what is full or fill what is empty, or that accomplish some other such thing. For one who has been chilled—not only a physician, but any layman whatever—led by the very nature of the matter, desires to find a heating drug, and likewise one who is being burnt up by a burning fever desires a cooling one. So too any layman whatever who has a sodden ulcer bids the physician to dry it, and, if it should be dry and moistureless, to moisten it. And indeed, when they perceive dryness together with heat throughout the whole body, as do both those who have been scorched by the sun and those who have been worn out by fatigue, they long to bathe and they drink cold water, and whatever may be capable of moistening and cooling at once, this they contrive to find out; and many of the laymen, led by nature itself, often procure for themselves the remedies for these conditions.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς ἤδη τὸ πέρας τοῦ μετρίου κεχαλασμένον, πλαδαρὸν, ἐγκαυθέντες, καυσώδους
The use of drugs for human beings often arises simply for this alone, namely their heating or cooling or moistening or drying, or their acting according to some combination of these; but sometimes it arises for their tightening and drawing together what has already been relaxed beyond the limit of the moderate, 707 or relaxing what has been tensed, or rarefying what has been condensed, or condensing what is porous, or softening what is hard, or hardening immoderate softnesses, or emptying what is full, or filling what is empty, or effecting some other such thing. For a person who has been chilled, not only the physician but any layman whatever, led by the very nature of the matter, desires to find a heating drug, and likewise one parched by a fever of the ardent (causus) kind desires a cooling one. So too any layman whatever who has a sodden ulcer bids the physician dry it, and if it is dry and without moisture, moisten it. And indeed, when they perceive dryness together with heat throughout the whole body, as those who have been scorched by the sun and those who have been fatigued long to bathe and drink cold water, they contrive to find whatever is capable of moistening and cooling at once; and many of the laymen, led by nature itself, do often provide themselves with the remedies for these conditions.

K11.707-708K. XI 707–708, ch. 5.2 difficulty high (4.19/100; thin 4, absent 3) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
φλεγμονῆς μέντοι καὶ σκίῤῥου καὶ οἰδήματος, ἐρυσιπέλατός τε καὶ σηπεδόνος, ἕρπητός τε καὶ γαγγραίνης, οὐκέτ’ οὐδεὶς 708 αὐτῶν ἐπιχειρεῖ ζητεῖν τὴν ἴασιν ἑαυτῷ, ὡς μείζονος ἤδη τῶν τοιούτων ἑκάστου ὑπάρχοντος ἢ κατὰ γνώμην ἰδιώτου καί τινος ἐπιστήμης σεμνοτέρας δεομένου ὀνομάζουσι δὲ τὴν τοιαύτην ἐπιστήμην ἰατρικὴν καὶ τὸν ἐργαζόμενον αὐτὴν ἰατρόν. καὶ μέχρι μὲν τοσούτου καὶ αὐτοὶ προΐασιν ὡς γινώσκειν ὅτι τὸ μὲν κοῖλον ἕλκος δεῖται σαρκώσεος, τὸ δὲ ῥυπαρὸν καθάρσεως, τὸ δ’ ὁμαλὸν ἐπουλώσεως· οὐ μὴν τό γε σαρκοῦν ἢ καθαῖρον ἢ ἐπουλοῦν φάρμακον ἴσασιν. οὕτω δὲ κᾂν εἰ μῦν ἢ τένοντα σκληρὸν ἢ τεταμένον ἢ χαλαρὸν ἔχοιεν, ὅτι τῷ μὲν σκληρῷ χρεία τῶν μαλαττόντων ἐστὶν, τῷ δὲ τεταμένῳ τῶν χαλώντων, τῷ δὲ χαλαρῷ τῶν συντεινόντων, ἀκριβῶς ἴσασιν, οὐ μὴν τό γε μαλάττον ἢ χαλῶν ἢ συντεῖνον ἐπίστανται φάρμακον, ἀλλ’ ἰατροῦ μόνου τὰ τοιαῦτα εὑρίσκειν ἔργον. ἄχρι μὲν γὰρ τῶν θερμαινόντων καὶ ψυχόντων, ὑγραινόντων τε καὶ ξηραινόντων, ἐστὶν εὑρεῖν ἐνίους τῶν ἰδιωτῶν κοινωνοῦντας τῆς τῶν φαρμάκων εὑρέσεως τοῖς ἰατροῖς, ἐν οἷς δ’ εἴρηται νῦν, ἤδη πάντας ἀπείρως ἔχοντας.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Of phlegmone, however, and of scirrhus and oedema, of erysipelas and putrefaction, of herpes and gangrene, no longer does any one 708 of them attempt to seek the cure for himself, since each of such conditions is already greater than the understanding of a layman and requires some more august science; and they name such a science medicine, and the one who practises it a physician. And they themselves advance thus far, as to know that a hollow ulcer needs flesh-production, a foul one cleansing, and a level one cicatrization; yet they do not know the drug that produces flesh or cleanses or cicatrizes. In the same way too, if they should have a muscle or tendon that is hard or tense or slack, they know precisely that the hard one has need of softening agents, the tense one of relaxing agents, and the slack one of tightening agents; yet they do not know the drug that softens or relaxes or tightens, but to discover such things is the work of the physician alone. For as far as heating and cooling, moistening and drying agents are concerned, one can find some of the laymen sharing with the physicians in the discovery of drugs; but in the matters now stated, all are already without experience.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς μείζονος ἤδη τῶν τοιούτων ἑκάστου ὑπάρχοντος ἢ κατὰ γνώμην ἰδιώτου, σεμνοτέρας, ἐπουλώσεως
Yet for inflammation and scirrhus and oedema, for erysipelas and putrefaction, for herpes and gangrene, none 708 of them any longer attempts to seek the cure for himself, since each such condition is by now too great a matter for a layman's judgement and requires some more august knowledge; and they call such knowledge medicine, and the one who practises it a physician. They themselves advance only so far as to know that the hollow ulcer needs filling with flesh, the foul ulcer cleansing, and the level ulcer cicatrization; but they do not know the drug that fills with flesh, or cleanses, or cicatrizes. In the same way, if they have a muscle or a tendon that is hard or tense or slack, they know precisely that the hard one has need of softening drugs, the tense one of relaxing drugs, and the slack one of tightening drugs; but they do not know the drug that softens or relaxes or tightens. Finding such things is the work of the physician alone. For as far as heating and cooling, moistening and drying drugs go, one can find some of the laymen sharing with the physicians in the discovery of drugs; but in the matters just mentioned, all of them are entirely without experience.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Concerning such matters of power, then, I have gone through in the four treatises before this one: in the first and second, refuting the arguments of the sophists and showing the true method by using which one might find the powers of drugs; in the third, having taken leave of the sophists, I went through from the beginning all the logical inquiries, by going through which especially, and being, as one might say, trained by them, one will be capable of finding the powers of all drugs; and in the fourth I made my discourse about the objects of sense proper to the 710 tongue, which indeed they name flavours (χυμοί), demonstrating how one, setting out from there, might find the primary qualities and powers. I also touched, to the extent that need called for it, on the odorous qualities at the end of that book, showing how much it is possible to take from there too for the discovery of the primary powers. But in this present discourse, the fifth, another kind of powers is set before me to expound—second, as one might say, and third, after the primary ones that are common to all. For since each of the particular drugs is not mixed in the same manner out of those, one of them, I think, has become relaxing, another tensing or softening or hardening or rarefying or condensing.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τεχνωθεὶς, ὑπομνήμασι
Concerning the power of such things I have treated in the four commentaries preceding this one: in the first and second, refuting the arguments of the sophists and showing the true method, by using which one might discover the powers of drugs; in the third, having left the sophists aside, I went through from the beginning all the logical questions, by going through which most thoroughly and, as one might say, by being made skilled in the art (technōtheis), one will be competent to discover the powers of all drugs; and in the fourth I made my discourse concerning the sensibles proper to the 710 tongue, which they call flavours (chymoi), showing how someone setting out from there might discover the primary qualities and powers. I also touched, so far as need called for it, on the olfactory qualities at the end of the book, showing how much it is possible to obtain from that source too towards the discovery of the primary powers. But in this present fifth book I propose to expound another class of powers, secondary, as one might say, and tertiary, in addition to the primary ones that are common to all things. For since each of the particular things is not mixed from those in the same way, one of them, I think, has become relaxing, another tightening or softening or hardening or rarefying or condensing.

Further, on the basis of the actions which they are naturally fitted to perform, they were said to have in them powers that are rarefying and condensing, softening and hardening, clogging and cleansing, drawing and repelling, and in addition to these relaxing, tensing, mouth-opening, contracting, thickening, thinning, pain-relieving, pain-producing, concocting, suppurating, dispersing, 711 sweat-producing, stupefying, numbing, sleep-inducing, deranging, putrefying, burning, corroding, eschar-forming, liquefying, compacting, productive of bad humours, tempering, purging, checking, roughening, smoothing, blocking and unblocking. And as people proceed still further and name them, as it were, individually according to each of the effects: diuretic, emetic, gently evacuating, laxative, errhine, phlegm-expelling, promoting or checking the menses. And likewise, of milk and of seed, either generative or extinguishing, either provoking or checking. And still more, when they name certain powers hepatic or splenic or otic or ophthalmic or dental or sciatic or nephritic or podagric or arthritic or pleuritic or cough-relieving or stone-crushing, they arrive at the particular ones. In the same manner it has also been said that there is a certain flesh-forming power for an ulcer, and a scarring one, and an agglutinating one, and a reducing one; and it is almost not even possible to count all the particular powers, if we attempt to name them after each of the effects produced.

And still more, when they name certain powers hepatic or splenic or aural or ophthalmic or dental or sciatic or nephritic or podagric or arthritic or pleuritic or for cough or stone-breaking, they arrive at the particular powers. In the same way it has also been said that there is a flesh-forming power for an ulcer, and a scarring one, and an agglutinating and a removing one. Indeed, it is scarcely even possible to enumerate all the particular powers if we attempt to name them after each of the things they bring about.

But it is also, 712 I think, both finer and more methodical, having withdrawn from the long and disorderly account, to define the composite powers of drugs by useful kinds, not saying that in the poultice made with wheat flour there is a suppurating or soothing or pain-relieving or dispersing or relaxing power, but that it is moderately moist and hot. But indeed 712 I think it finer and more methodical to withdraw from the long and disorderly account and to define the composite powers of drugs by useful classes: not saying that in the poultice made with wheaten meal there is a suppurating, or soothing, or anodyne, or dispersing, or relaxing power, but that it is moderately moist and warm. And in the same way, nor that it is suited for gonorrhoea, or that it is usefully mixed into fomentations of the womb, as softening and opening the mouths of vessels; nor indeed that it cleanses away lentil-spots or freckles 713 and cures chronic headache, or that it is naturally disposed to destroy embryos and draw them down. And in another way again, it suffices to say of it that it is bitter, yet not extremely so, but such that some sweetness seems to be mixed in. The first manner of teaching, then, is peculiar to the Empiricists, whereas the second befits above all a rational man, and it is this one that we now propose to go through. All the other matters discussed previously throughout the four commentaries are proper to the theory of drugs itself, except where, by way of community, the discussion touched upon the therapeutic method. But the matter now at hand belongs rather to that discipline, so that I often deliberated whether to pass over it entirely.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συνάγοντά
But because it seemed good to me to train in advance those who are going to follow the reasonings of the therapeutic method, and because most of my companions, and especially 714 those to gratify whom I came to this treatise, asked me to carry it through in this way, for this reason I shall take up the discussion in this book, making my beginning from the suppurating and the softening drugs; since both are hot and moist, yet each in a different manner. For the former are so inasmuch as they generate a heat most similar to the body that is being brought to suppuration, in addition to neither removing anything of the moisture that is present in it nor adding to it; the latter inasmuch as they kindle a heat even greater than the natural one and draw together some of the moisture. Now the notion of the suppurating drugs is at once clear and familiar to everyone; for suppuration is a change into pus. But concerning the softening drugs and softening itself it is no longer possible to speak so simply, because it is not possible to do so concerning the hard and the soft body either.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: συνάγοντα, διαμαλάξεως
But since it seemed good to me to give a preliminary training to those who are going to follow the reasonings of the therapeutic method, and since most of my companions, and especially 714 those as a favour to whom I came to this treatise, asked me to do so, for this reason I shall take up the account in this book, making the beginning from the drugs that bring to suppuration and those that soften. For both are hot and moist, but each in a different way. The former generate a heat most similar to that of the body being brought to suppuration, and besides neither take away any of the moisture present in it nor add any. The latter kindle even more heat than the natural and gather together some of the moisture. Now the conception of the drugs that bring to suppuration is at once clear and familiar to all, for suppuration is a change into pus. About softening drugs and about softening itself, however, it is no longer possible to speak so simply, because neither is it possible about the hard and the soft body.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: παραβαλόντα
since indeed one thing is called hard simply, as earth is; another by predominance, as nail and spur and horn; another in relation to the well-proportioned member of the same genus or of the same species, when one makes the comparison in the manner of this particular animal, such as an elephant, or this particular man, such as Heracles; 715 and another as compared with anything whatever, as Diogenes compared with Aristotle. In the same way the soft too is spoken of either as being such in the extreme degree, or by predominance, or in relation to the well-proportioned member of the same genus or of the same species, or in relation to anything whatever that happens to come to hand. And that it is also possible to speak of all such things in three ways—some as possessing unmixed and extreme the qualities from which they derive their names, some as relative to the well-proportioned member of the same genus or of the same species, and some as compared with anything whatever that happens to come to hand—and that it makes no difference whether one speaks in the former way or in the latter, has already been demonstrated by me many times.

Greek (Kühn)
ἐάσαντες οὖν τὰ λοιπὰ πάντα σημαινόμενα περὶ τῶν πρὸς τὸν ἄριστα κατεσκευασμένον ἄνθρωπον ἐπισκεψώμεθα μαλακῶν καὶ σκληρῶν, ὃν δὴ κανόνα καὶ μέτρον ἐποιησάμεθα τῶν οὕτω λεγομένων ἁπάντων, οὐδὲ τούτου πρὸς τὸ ἐπιτυχὸν μόριον, οἷον ὀστοῦν ἢ πιμελὴν, ἀλλὰ τὸ μέσον τῇ κράσει, τὸ δέρμα καὶ τούτου μάλιστα τὸ κατὰ τὰς χεῖρας, ἵνα περ ἡ ἁπτικὴ δύναμις ἀκριβοῦται· συμβαίνει δ’ εἰς ταὐτὸ καὶ τὸ κατ’ ἐπικράτησιν, ὡς πρὸς τὴν ὅλην οὐσίαν, οὕτως ὀνομάζεσθαι μαλακὸν ἢ σκληρὸν, 716 ἐπειδὴ μεσότης τίς ἐστιν ἡμῶν ἡ ἁφὴ, καθ’ ὃ καὶ Ἀριστοτέλης ἐδίδασκεν. οὕτω δέ πως καὶ ὁ Πλάτων ἔοικε γιγνώσκειν ὅταν ἔφη· σκληρὰ μὲν ὅσοις ἂν ἡμῶν σὰρξ ὑπείκῃ, μαλακὰ δ’ ὅσα ἂν ὑπείκῃ τῇ σαρκί. τὸ γὰρ δέρμα τοῦ ἀνθρώπου κατ’ αὐτὸ τοῦτο τὸ σύγγραμμα τὸν Τίμαιον ἐδείκνυεν εἶναι τῷ γένει σάρκα. καὶ μὲν δὴ καὶ Ἀριστοτέλης, ὅταν εἶπε μαλακὸν μὲν εἶναι τὸ ὑπεῖκον εἰς αὐτὸ, σκληρὸν δὲ τὸ μὴ ὑπεῖκον, ὡσαύτως ἔοικεν ἀποφαίνεσθαι τῷ Πλάτωνι, καὶ μάλιστα ὅτι πρὸς τὴν ἁφὴν τὰ τοιαῦτα κρίνεσθαί φησιν, ὡς πρὸς μεσότητα. τὸ δὲ ἐν τῇ πρὸς ὁτιοῦν παραβολῇ σκληρὸν ἢ μαλακὸν ἐν τῷ μᾶλλον ἢ ἧττον ὑπείκειν ἢ μὴ ὑπείκειν φαίνεται κρίνεσθαι. καὶ δὴ καὶ αὐτὸ τὸ δέρμα τὸ ἡμέτερον ἢ τὸν μῦν ἤ τι μόριον ἕτερον, ὅταν εἴπωμεν ἐσκληρύνθαι, τῇ κατὰ φύσιν αὐτοῦ καταστάσει τὴν ἐπίκτητον διάθεσιν παραβάλλοντες οὕτως ὀνομάζομεν. ἔστι δὲ δήπου τῶν οὕτως ἐχόντων σωμάτων ἡ εἰς τὸ κατὰ φύσιν ἐπάνοδος μάλαξις.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Leaving aside, then, all the remaining significations, let us examine the soft and hard things [as spoken of] in relation to the best-constituted human being, whom indeed we have made the rule and measure of all things so called—and not [in relation to] any chance part of him, such as bone or soft fat, but the part that is intermediate in mixture, the skin, and of this above all the part on the hands, where precisely the tactile faculty is at its most exact. And it comes to the same thing that something is named soft or hard according to predominance, with reference to the whole substance, 716 since our touch is a kind of mean, as Aristotle too taught. And Plato too seems to hold roughly this view when he said: "hard are those things to which our flesh yields, soft those which yield to the flesh." For in this very treatise, the Timaeus, he showed the skin of the human being to be flesh in kind. And indeed Aristotle too, when he said that the soft is what yields into itself and the hard what does not yield, seems to declare the same as Plato, especially in that he says such things are judged with reference to touch, as with reference to a mean. But the hard or soft in the comparison with anything whatever appears to be judged by yielding or not yielding more or less. And indeed when we say that our own skin itself, or a muscle, or some other part has been hardened, we name it thus by comparing its acquired condition with its natural state. And surely, for bodies in such a state, the return to the natural condition is softening.

Leaving aside, then, all the other meanings, let us examine the things that are soft and hard relative to the best-constituted human being, whom we have made the canon and measure of all things so called. And we do not measure relative to any chance part of him, such as bone or fat, but relative to the part intermediate in its mixture, the skin, and of this especially the skin of the hands, where the tactile faculty is brought to its greatest precision. It also turns out that what is so named soft or hard by predominance, as relative to the whole substance, comes to the same thing, 716 since our touch is a kind of mean, as Aristotle also taught. Plato too seems to have held much the same view when he said: 'hard are those things to which our flesh yields, soft those that yield to the flesh.' For in this very treatise, the Timaeus, he showed that the skin of the human being is flesh in kind. And indeed Aristotle too, when he said that soft is what yields into itself and hard what does not yield, seems to declare the same as Plato, especially since he says that such things are judged relative to touch, as relative to a mean. But what is hard or soft in comparison with anything whatever appears to be judged by its yielding or not yielding more or less. And indeed when we say that our skin itself, or a muscle, or some other part, has become hardened, we name it so by comparing the acquired condition with its natural state. And the return of bodies in this condition to their natural state is, of course, softening.

But since each thing becomes harder in many ways — either being dried, or being congealed, or being overfilled 717 so as to be violently distended, or being affected according to some conjunction of these — the softening too, in the case of each of them, will be specific to it. Let us then draw distinctions concerning all of them in order, first recalling that an easy-going attitude about nouns and verbs is for the most part not ignoble, but rather its opposite is illiberal, whenever it is not necessary to make distinctions about them. For so Plato said, and for us now, for the present occasion, it is necessary to make distinctions about the meanings of 'hard', beginning again from the same points which we stated just now. For what yields into itself is soft, provided that it is a simple body; but what is composed of several things, either touching one another, like a heap of wheat-grains, or somehow interwoven, like a fleece or plaits (πλόκαμα), could yield and at the same time not be soft.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Conversely, in the case of things that have been completely filled, such as wineskins or bladders, the two together do not yield into themselves, yet neither is what is contained hard; nor, for that matter, has the container, once it has been filled, become harder than it was or taken on some other acquired condition, 718 except only that it has become stretched from having previously been slack. Such a thing, then, I for my part call resistant and not hard, whenever I choose to speak with precision. I concede, however, if anyone should wish to name it hard as well, and I myself often call it so, reminding only of this: that there is neither one nature of things that have been hardened nor one remedy. For what has been hardened by dryness needs to be moistened, what by congealing to be heated, what by filling to be evacuated, what by dryness together with congealing to be both moistened and heated, and what by congealing together with filling to be both heated and evacuated. Bodies, then, are dried without congealing in immoderate exercises, in strong sun, in severe deprivations, in burning fever, and by certain drugs, namely those that dry without cooling. They are congealed by strong cooling alone, just as they are also filled through an influx of abundant moisture. And they are dried and congealed at the same time when causes capable of coexisting concur in the same thing, as for instance if someone should overexert himself in the cold.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δυναμέων (apparently corrupt; read as δυναμένων, 'able'), ἐκ τοῦ τέως κεχαλασμένου, πυρετῷ καυσώδει, ἐνδείαις
Conversely, in things that are filled completely, such as wineskins or bladders, the two together do not yield into themselves; yet the contents are certainly not hard, nor has the container, when it has been filled, become harder than it was, or taken on any other acquired condition, 718 except that it has merely been stretched from its previously slack state. Such a thing, then, I call resistant and not hard, whenever I choose to speak with precision. I concede, however, that if anyone wishes he may call it hard too, and I myself often call it so, reminding only of this: that there is neither one nature of hardened things nor one remedy. For what has been hardened by dryness needs to be moistened; what by congelation, to be warmed; what by filling, to be emptied; what by dryness and congelation together, to be both moistened and warmed; and what by congelation and filling together, to be both warmed and emptied. Bodies, then, are dried without congelation in immoderate exercises, in intense sun, in severe deprivations, in a causus-like fever, and by certain drugs, namely those that dry without cooling. They are congealed by strong cooling alone, just as they are also filled through an influx of abundant moisture. They are dried and congealed at the same time when causes that are able to coexist converge upon the same thing, as for instance if someone were to overexert himself in frost.

οὕτω δὲ καὶ πληροῦται καὶ πήγνυται διὰ ῥεῦμα ﻿ 719 ψυχρὸν ἤ τινα ψύξιν τοῦ μορίου. τῆς ψύξεως δ’ αὖ πάλιν αἰτία τριττὴ, μία μὲν ἐκ τῶν ἔξωθεν προσπιπτόντων, οἷον ἀέρος, ὕδατος, ἢ φαρμάκου, καθάπερ ἐπὶ τῶν ἐρυσιπελάτων ἐργάζονται πολλάκις, ἑτέρα δὲ ἡ τοῦ πάσχοντος μέρους οἰκεία κρᾶσις, ἄλλη δ’ ἐπ’ αὐταῖς τρίτη παρὰ τοῦ ῥυέντος εἰς αὐτὸ χυμοῦ γιγνομένη, πολλάκις γὰρ ἐκεῖνος μὲν ἤδη θερμότερος ἑαυτοῦ γέγονεν, ἤτοι σηπόμενος ἢ ἐκ τῶν ψαυόντων αὐτῷ μορίων τῶν φύσει θερμῶν μεταβαλλόμενος, ἢν δὲ προσετρίψατό τινι μορίῳ τῶν φύσει ψυχροτέρων ψύξιν, ἕως πολλοῦ παραμένει. κατὰ τοσούτους μὲν τρόπους σκληρύνεταί τε καὶ μαλάττεται τὰ σώματα. τὸ μέντοι μαλακτικὸν φάρμακον οὐκ ἐπὶ πάντας ἔοικε φέρειν τοὺς τρόπους, ἀλλ’ ἐξαιρέτως ἐπὶ τοὺς διὰ πῆξιν ἐσκληρυμμένους, ἔτι δὲ μᾶλλον, εἰ καὶ περιεχομένη τις ὑγρότης ἐν αὐτοῖς εἴη παρὰ φύσιν, ὡς ἐπὶ τῶν ἐσκιῤῥωμένων. εἰ γὰρ διὰ ξηρότητα σκληρὸν εἴη γεγονὸς, ὑγραίνειν μᾶλλον, οὐ μαλάττειν τὸν τοιοῦτον κελεύουσιν, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τὰ πεπληρωμένα κενοῦν, οὐ μαλάττειν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προσετρίψατό
So too it is both filled and congealed through a flux 719 that is cold, or through some chilling of the part. And of the chilling, in turn, the cause is threefold: one from the things that impinge from outside, such as air, water, or a drug, as they often bring it about in cases of erysipelas; another, the proper mixture of the affected part; and another, third, in addition to these, arising from the humour that has flowed into it. For often that humour has already become hotter than it was, either because it is putrefying or because it is altered by the parts in contact with it that are hot by nature; but the chilling which it imparted to some part of those that are colder by nature persists for a long time. In so many ways, then, are bodies hardened and softened. The softening drug, however, seems not to bear upon all these ways, but especially upon those that have been hardened through congelation, and still more if there is also some moisture contained in them contrary to nature, as in the case of parts that have become scirrhous. For if a thing has become hard through dryness, they bid one moisten, not soften, such a thing, just as also they bid one evacuate, not soften, what has been filled.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἢν (read as relative ἣν referring to ψύξιν), προσετρίψατο, ἔοικε φέρειν, τὸν τοιοῦτον
In the same way, bodies are both filled and congealed through a cold flux 719 or some chilling of the part. Of the chilling, in turn, the cause is threefold: one from the things that fall upon it from outside, such as air, water or a drug, as they often bring it about in the case of erysipelases; another, the proper mixture of the affected part; and a third besides these, arising from the humour that has flowed into it. For often that humour has itself already become hotter than it was, either by putrefying or by being changed by the parts touching it that are hot by nature; but the chilling which it has imparted to some one of the parts that are colder by nature persists for a long time. In so many ways, then, bodies are both hardened and softened. The softening drug, however, does not seem to apply to all of these ways, but specifically to those hardened through congelation, and still more if some moisture contrary to nature is also contained in them, as in the case of things that have become scirrhous. For if something has become hard through dryness, they bid one rather to moisten such a case, not to soften it, just as also to empty, not to soften, things that are filled.

Greek (Kühn)
ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τῶν ὀνομάτων οὔτ’ ἐρίζειν 720 καλὸν οὔτ’ ἀκριβοῦν ἀναγκαῖον. ἐπὶ δὲ τὰς ἐν τοῖς πράγμασι διαφορὰς ἰέναι βέλτιον, ἐν αἷς καὶ τὸ σφάλμα μεγάλην τοῖς ἀῤῥωστοῦσι φέρει τὴν βλάβην. τὸ μὲν οὖν ἐξηρασμένον τῶν ὑγραινόντων δεῖται φαρμάκων, ὑπὲρ ὧν καὶ ἤδη μοι πρόσθεν αὐτάρκως εἴρηται, τὸ δὲ πεπηγὸς ὑπὸ ψύξεως θερμαινόντων. οὐκ ἀγνοεῖται δὲ οὐδὲ ταῦτα, τὸ μέντοι γε πεπληρωμένον ἢ ψυχόντων ἢ θερμαινόντων ἢ τῶν ἰδίως ὀνομαζομένων ξηραινόντων. ἐκκενοῖ γὰρ τὴν περιττὴν ὑγρότητα ταῦτα σύμπαντα, κατ’ ἴδιον ἕκαστον λόγον. ὅσα μὲν ψύχει κατὰ διττὸν τρόπον, ἀποκρουόμενά τε ἅμα καὶ συνεξάγοντα τῷ θερμῷ νοτίδα πολλὴν, ὡς Ἀριστοτέλης ἐδίδασκεν. ὅσα δὲ θερμαίνει, τῷ λύειν εἰς ἀτμοὺς μὲν τὸ περιεχόμενον ὑγρὸν ἐν τοῖς θερμαινομένοις σώμασιν, ὅσα δὲ ξηραίνει, καθάπερ τὰ διαφορητικὰ προσαγορευόμενα, κατὰ διττὸν καὶ ταῦτα τρόπον, ἢ ἀναπίνοντα τὰς ὑγρότητας, ὅταν ἐν πόροις τισὶν ὑπάρχωσιν, ἢ ὅλον ἀλλοιοῦντα τὸ μόριον. ὅστις δ’ ἑκάστου ὁ καιρὸς τῆς χρήσεως οὐ τῆς ἐνεστώσης πραγματείας, ἀλλὰ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς ἐστι μεθόδου.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But concerning names it is neither fine to quarrel 720 nor necessary to be exact. It is better to proceed to the differences in the things themselves, in which error also brings great harm to the sick. What has been dried out, then, needs moistening drugs, about which I have already spoken sufficiently earlier; what has been congealed by cooling needs heating ones. Nor are these things unknown either; but what has been filled needs either cooling drugs or heating ones or those properly called drying. For all of these evacuate the surplus moisture, each according to its own principle. Those that cool do so in a twofold manner, at once repelling and at the same time drawing out, together with the heat, much moisture, as Aristotle taught. Those that heat, by dissolving into vapours the moisture contained in the bodies that are being heated; and those that dry, like those called dispersing, these too in a twofold manner, either drinking up the moistures when they are present in certain pores, or altering the whole part. But what the right moment for the use of each is belongs not to the present treatise but to the therapeutic method.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: βουλιμιῶν
But those 721 that have been hardened by cooling together with dryness, it is fitting to heat and at the same time to moisten, not in the way that suppurating parts are treated, with heat and moisture that are moderate according to nature; rather, the drug must be hotter to the extent that the cooling has prevailed, and moister to the extent that the dryness has; and to define the measure of these too belongs to the therapeutic method. Here it will suffice to go through a certain outline of the form of the drugs by means of a few examples. Hot water, then, moistens and heats, and so does oil. And if both are mixed together into the same thing, the mixture made from them will be much more moistening and at the same time heating, just as are also baths of drinkable waters with abundant oil. This same thing is produced also by the quality of foods when it is of the same sort; for this reason too the remedies for attacks of boulimos happen to be of this kind. For the affection involves cooling together with dryness, and their cure is through the opposites.

But those parts that 721 have been hardened by cold and dryness together ought to be warmed and moistened at the same time; not in the way that parts that have suppurated are treated, with heat and moisture together that are moderate and in accordance with nature, but the drug must be warmer by as much as the cold has prevailed, and moister by as much as the dryness has; and defining the measure of these too belongs to the therapeutic method. Here it will be enough to go through a sort of outline of the kind of drugs, using a few examples. Hot water and oil, then, moisten and warm. And if both are mixed together into one, the mixture made from them will be much more moistening and warming at once, just as baths of potable waters with abundant oil are. The quality of foods, when it is in the same condition, also produces this very same effect; for this reason the remedies for cases of boulimia have also turned out to be of this sort. For the affection involves cold together with dryness, and their cure is through contraries.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
The poultice made with wheat flour, however, which brings well-tempered natures to suppuration, neither removes anything worth mentioning of the natural moisture in the parts 722, and much less does it add any; just as it is also of a nature neither to intensify nor to blunt the warmth, but only to increase it in respect of its substance. And it is not the same thing to intensify the quality and to increase the substance. These matters too will be shown more precisely a little later. Such drugs, then, we call moist and hot, not as being moister and hotter than our substance, but as being of a mixture similar to ours, which is moist and hot according to the ancient account, as we said in the work On Mixtures. It is not in this way, however, that we say that water is moist or galbanum hot, but the former as wetting and soaking our flesh, the latter as heating it. For even if the water be hot, so that it is sometimes able to empty the parts that have been raised into a swelling through an influx of humours, it nonetheless in every case moistens the homoeomerous parts, as has also been shown by me in the first book.

The poultice made from wheaten meal, however, which brings well-tempered natures to suppuration, neither draws off from the natural moisture in the parts 722 anything worth mentioning, nor, still less, adds any. In the same way it does nothing to the warmth either: it is by nature disposed neither to intensify it nor to blunt it, but to increase it in respect of substance alone. Yet to intensify the quality and to increase the substance are not the same thing. This too will be shown more precisely a little later. Drugs of this kind, then, we call moist and warm. We do not mean that they are moister and warmer than our own substance, but that they are of a mixture closely similar to ours, which is moist and warm according to the ancient account, as we said in the work On Mixtures. It is not, however, in this sense that we say water is moist or galbanum warm. We call the former moist as wetting and soaking through our flesh, and the latter warm as warming it. For even if the water be warm, so that it is sometimes able to empty parts raised into a swelling through an influx of fluids, it at any rate certainly moistens the homoeomerous parts, as has been shown by me in the first book as well.

Greek (Kühn)
Τὰ τοίνυν ἐκπυΐσκοντα φάρμακα τῷ μὲν θερμαίνειν ὁμοίως ὕδατι θερμῷ διαφορεῖ τὴν περιττὴν 723 ὑγρότητα, τὴν ἐν ταῖς κεναῖς χώραις περιεχομένην, ὡς ἐν τῷ περὶ τῆς ἀνωμάλου δυσκρασίας ἐπιδέδεικται γράμματι. τοῖς μέντοι κατὰ φύσιν ἔχουσι σώμασιν αὐτοῖς τοῖς ὁμοιομερέσιν οὐδὲν προστίθησιν ὑγρότητος, ὥσπερ οὐδ’ ἀφαιρεῖ τι σαφὲς, οὐδ’ αἰσθητόν. ἶσα γὰρ ὑπάρχοντα ταῖς κράσεσι φυλακτικὰ τῆς οὐσίας αὐτῶν ἐστιν, ὡς κατὰ μηδὲν ἀλλοιοῦν. ἐν γοῦν ταῖς ἐκπυήσεσιν ἡ μὲν ὑγρότης ἀλλοιοῦται, κᾂν εἴ πού τις εἴη σὰρξ τεθλασμένη, τὰ δ’ ἄλλα πάντα τὰ κατὰ φύσιν ἔχοντα διασώζει τὴν ἑαυτῶν οὐσίαν. τριῶν γὰρ γινομένων ἀλλοιώσεων ἐν τοῖς τῶν ζώων σώμασιν, ἡ μέν τις ἀκριβῶς ἐστι κατὰ φύσιν ἔχουσα, ὅταν ἐν τῇ γαστρὶ πέττηται τὸ σιτίον ἢ ἐν τοῖς σπλάγχνοις καὶ ἀγγείοις ὁ γεννηθεὶς ἐνταῦθα χυλὸς, ἵνα ἐκ τούτου πάλιν τρέφηται ἕκαστον μέλος· ἡ δέ τις ἀκριβῶς παρὰ φύσιν ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς σηπομένοις. αὗται μὲν οὖν ἐναντίαι πως ἀλλήλαις εἰσίν. ἡ τρίτη δ’ ἐπίμικτος ἐξ ἀμφοῖν καὶ μέση, τὸ μέν τι τῆς πρώτης τε καὶ κατὰ φύσιν ἔχουσα, τὸ δὲ τῆς ἐναντίας αὐτῆς τῆς παρὰ φύσιν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: προστίθησιν (singular verb with plural subject φάρμακα)
The drugs that bring about suppuration, then, by their heating, like hot water, disperse the surplus 723 moisture, that which is contained in the empty spaces, as has been demonstrated in the treatise On Uneven Dyscrasia. To bodies in their natural state, however, in the homoeomerous parts themselves, it adds no moisture, just as it does not remove anything clear or perceptible. For being equal to them in their mixtures, they are preservative of their substance, so as to alter it in no respect. At any rate, in suppurations the moisture is altered, and also if somewhere there is bruised flesh, but all the other parts which are in their natural state preserve their own substance. For since three alterations occur in the bodies of animals, one is precisely according to nature, when the food is concocted in the stomach, or when the juice generated there is concocted in the viscera and vessels, so that from this in turn each member is nourished; another is precisely contrary to nature, in all things that are putrefying. These, then, are in a way opposite to each other. The third is mixed from both and intermediate, having something of the first, the one according to nature, and something of its opposite, the one contrary to nature.

Drugs that bring to suppuration, then, by heating in the same way as hot water, disperse the superfluous 723 moisture, the moisture contained in the empty spaces, as has been demonstrated in the treatise On Uneven Bad Mixture. To bodies that are in their natural state, however, to the homoeomerous bodies themselves, they add nothing of moisture, just as they also take away nothing evident or perceptible. For since they are equal to them in their mixtures, they are preservative of their substance, so as to alter it in no respect. In suppurations, at any rate, the moisture is altered, and also any crushed flesh there may be, but all the other things that are in their natural state preserve their own substance. For of the three alterations that occur in the bodies of animals, one is strictly in accordance with nature: when food is concocted in the stomach, or when the juice (chylos) generated there is concocted in the viscera and vessels, so that from it in turn each member may be nourished. Another is strictly contrary to nature, in all things that putrefy. These, then, are in a way opposite to one another. The third is admixed from both and intermediate, having something of the first, the one in accordance with nature, and something of its opposite, the one contrary to nature.

ὑπάρχει γὰρ τῇ μὲν κατὰ φύσιν δύο ταῦτα, ﻿ 724 τό τε ἐξ οἰκείας ὕλης τῷ ζώῳ τὴν ἀλλοίωσιν γίγνεσθαι, τό θ’ ὑπὸ τῆς ἐμφύτου θερμότητος ἀκριβῶς κρατεῖσθαι. τῇ δὲ παρὰ φύσιν ὑπ’ ἀλλοτρίας τε θερμότητος ἡ μεταβολὴ καὶ εἰς οὐδὲν χρηστόν· ἡ μέση δ’ αὐτῶν ἡ κατὰ τὰς ἐμπυήσεις ἑπομένη ὑπὸ μὲν τῆς ἐμφύτου γίνεται θερμότητος, οὐ μὴν ἀκριβῶς γε κρατούσης· οὐδὲ γὰρ ἐξ ὕλης ἀκριβῶς χρηστῆς οὔσης ἐπιτελεῖται, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ παντάπασιν ἀλλοτρίας. ὡς οὖν αἱ κατὰ φύσιν ἀλλοιώσεις ὑπὸ τῆς ἐμφύτου γιγνόμεναι θερμότητος ὑπὸ τῆς ὁμοίας ἔξωθεν ἐπικουροῦνται, κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον καὶ ἡ περὶ τὰς διαπυήσεις. ἴσμεν γοῦν οὐδὲν οὕτως τῇ κατὰ γαστέρα πέψει συντελοῦν ὡς ἀνθρώπειον σῶμα ψαῦον αὐτῆς. καί τινες παιδία προστιθέμενοι νύκτωρ ἐναργεστάτης ὠφελείας αἰσθάνονται ἀναπαυόμενοι. συμφυλοτέρα γὰρ ἥδε καὶ οἰκειοτέρα πολὺ τῆς διὰ τῶν πυριάσεών ἐστιν. ἔνιοι δὲ καὶ κυνίδια σμικρὰ τῆς αὐτῆς ἕνεκεν ὠφελείας ἀναπαυόμενοι προστίθενται τῇ γαστρὶ, τὸ πλῆθος αὔξοντες τῆς πεττούσης τὰ σιτία θερμότητος, οὐ τὴν ποιότητα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For the one according to nature has these two features: 724 both that the alteration comes about from matter proper to the animal, and that it is completely mastered by the innate heat. The one contrary to nature, by contrast, has its change brought about by an alien heat, and to nothing useful; while the one intermediate between them, the one that follows in suppurations, is brought about by the innate heat, though not by one that masters completely; for neither is it accomplished out of matter that is completely useful, just as not out of matter that is altogether alien either. As, then, the alterations according to nature, being brought about by the innate heat, are assisted by a similar heat from outside, in the same way also is the alteration in suppurations. At any rate, we know of nothing that contributes so much to concoction in the belly as a human body touching it. And some people, applying children to themselves at night while resting, perceive a most evident benefit. For this heat is more akin and far more proper than that which comes through fomentations. And some people even apply small puppies to the belly while resting for the sake of the same benefit, increasing the quantity of the heat that concocts the foods, not its quality.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐμπυήσεις, διαπυήσεις, συμφυλοτέρα, πυριάσεων, ἡ μεταβολὴ καὶ εἰς οὐδὲν χρηστόν
For the natural one has these two features: 724 that the alteration takes place out of material proper to the animal, and that it is exactly mastered by the innate heat. In the unnatural one, the change is brought about by foreign heat and leads to nothing useful. The one intermediate between them, which accompanies suppurations (empyēseis), comes about through the innate heat, but a heat that does not exactly master it. For it is not accomplished out of material that is exactly useful, just as it is not accomplished out of material that is wholly foreign either. So, just as the natural alterations, which come about through the innate heat, are assisted by a similar heat from outside, in the same way the alteration in suppurations (diapyēseis) is assisted too. We know, at any rate, that nothing contributes so much to concoction in the stomach as a human body touching it. And some people, placing children against it at night while they rest, perceive a most evident benefit. For this heat is much more akin and more proper than that which comes through fomentations. Some people also, for the sake of the same benefit, place small puppies against the stomach while they rest, increasing the quantity of the heat that concocts the foods, not its quality.

τοιοῦτον οὖν εἶναι χρὴ καὶ τὸ τῇ διαπυήσει 725 συλληψόμενον φάρμακον, οἷόν περ καὶ αὐτὸ τὸ ἔμφυτον ὑπάρχει θερμὸν ἐν ταῖς εὐκράτοις φύσεσιν· εἰ δὲ θερμοτέρα τοῦ δέοντος ἡ φύσις ὑπάρχει, τὸ διαπυΐσκον φάρμακον ἐν ἐκείνῳ τῷ σώματι θερμότερον εἶναι δεήσει, καὶ τοσούτῳ γε τοῦ συμμέτρου θερμότερον, ὅσῳ καὶ ἡ φύσις ἐστὶ τῆς εὐκράτου θερμοτέρα, ὥστε καὶ τοῦτο μὲν ἤδη πρόδηλον ἐκ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς ὑπάρχει μεθόδου, τὸ καθ’ ἕκαστον ἄνθρωπον ἴδιον εἶναι τὸ διαπυΐσκον φάρμακον. οὐχ ἥκιστα δὲ καὶ τὸ πολλάκις ἡμῖν ἀποδεδειγμένον ὑπὸ τούτων μαρτυρεῖται, τὸ χρῆναι πάσας τῶν φαρμάκων τὰς δυνάμεις πρὸς τὸν ἄριστα κεκραμένον ἄνθρωπον ἐξετάζεσθαι· μηδὲ γὰρ συστῆναι δύνασθαι τέχνην μηδεμίαν, εἰ μὴ πρότερον οἷον κανόνα τινὰ καὶ σκοπὸν τῷ γένει τῆς ὕλης, ἐν ᾗ καταγίνεται, θέμενος πρὸς ἐκεῖνον ἤδη τὰ κατὰ μέρος ἀπευθύνοι πάντα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Such, then, must also be the drug that is going to assist 725 suppuration, of the very same kind as the innate heat itself is in well-tempered natures; but if the nature is hotter than it ought to be, the suppurating drug in that body will need to be hotter, and hotter than the moderate one by just so much as the nature too is hotter than the well-tempered one, so that this, for its part, is already evident from the therapeutic method, namely that the suppurating drug is peculiar to each individual human being. And not least is what has often been demonstrated by us also attested by these considerations, namely that all the powers of drugs must be examined with reference to the best-mixed human being; for no art whatsoever can be constituted, unless one first sets up, as it were, a certain rule and target for the kind of material in which it is engaged, and then directs all the particulars toward that.

Greek (Kühn)
πάλιν οὖν ὑποθέμενοι τῷ λόγῳ τὸν ἄριστα κεκραμένον ἄνθρωπον οἷον σκοπόν τινα, πρὸς ἐκεῖνον ἀναφέροντες, ἐκπυΐσκον τι λέγομεν εἶναι φάρμακον ἕτερον τοῦ μαλάττοντος τὰ σκιῤῥούμενα, τάς τε κράσεις αὐτῶν ὑπάρχειν οἵας ἔμπροσθεν ἔφαμεν 726 εἶναι, τοῦ μὲν εἰς πύου γένεσιν συντελοῦντος τῇ φύσει, σύμμετρόν τε καὶ ὁμοίαν ᾧ προσάγεται σώματι; τῷ δ’ εἶναι τὴν φύσιν ἡμῶν ὑγρὰν καὶ θερμὴν, ὑγρὰ καὶ θερμὰ τὰ τοιαῦτα πολλάκις ὀνομάζεσθαι φάρμακα, τοῦ δὲ μαλάττοντος τὸ σκιῤῥούμενον πολὺ μὲν θερμότερον τῆς εὐκράτου φύσεως, οὐ μὴν ἰσχυρῶς γε ἤδη θερμήν. Ἄρχεται μὲν γὰρ ἡ τῶν σκιῤῥουμένων διάθεσις ἐκ ῥεύματος γλίσχρου τε καὶ παχέος ἐν μικροῖς πόροις τοῦ μορίου σφηνωθέντος. διαφορηθείσης δὲ τῆς ἐν αὐτῷ λεπτομεροῦς ὑγρότητος, εἶτα τῆς ὑπολοίπου ψυχθείσης τε καὶ οἷον πῆξίν τινα λαβούσης, ἡ καλουμένη σκίῤῥωσις ἕπεται, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ ψυχρὸν εἶναι τὸ πάθος φασὶν καὶ ἴασις αὐτοῦ διὰ τῶν θερμαινόντων. ἀλλ’ ἐπεὶ μετὰ ψύξεως ἦν ὑγρότης περιττὴ, σύνθετον ἔσται καὶ τὸ ἴαμα, διὰ μὲν τὴν ψύξιν τῶν θερμαινόντων δεομένης τῆς διαθέσεως, διὰ δὲ τὴν ἀλλοτρίαν τε καὶ περιττὴν ὑγρότητα τῶν ἐκκενούντων.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σύμμετρόν τε καὶ ὁμοίαν ᾧ προσάγεται σώματι; (the question mark in Kühn's punctuation), οὐ μὴν ἰσχυρῶς γε ἤδη θερμήν (accusative θερμήν where a genitive agreeing with τοῦ μαλάττοντος or nominative would be expected)
Again, then, positing in our account the best-mixed human being as a kind of target, and referring to him, we say that a suppurative drug is something different from the one that softens parts that are becoming scirrhous, and that their mixtures are such as we said earlier 726 they were: that of the one which assists nature toward the generation of pus being well-proportioned and similar to the body to which it is applied; and, because our nature is moist and warm, such drugs are often called moist and warm; whereas that of the one which softens the scirrhous part is much warmer than the well-mixed nature, yet certainly not already strongly warm. For the condition of parts becoming scirrhous begins from a viscous and thick flux that has been wedged in the small pores of the part. When the fine-particled moisture in it has been dispersed, and then the remainder has been cooled and has taken on, as it were, a kind of congelation, what is called scirrhosis follows; and for this reason they say both that the affection is cold and that its cure is through warming agents. But since the surplus moisture was present together with the cooling, the remedy too will be composite: because of the cooling the condition needs warming agents, and because of the alien and surplus moisture, evacuating agents.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σώματι; (the printed ';' read as a raised stop, not a question mark), τῇ φύσει (construed with προσάγεται, 'applied by nature'; it could instead go with συντελοῦντος, 'contributes to nature')
Once more, then, positing in our account the best-mixed human being as a kind of target, and referring to him, we say that a suppurative drug is something different from one that softens indurated parts, and that their mixtures are such as we said 726 earlier: that of the drug which contributes to the generation of pus is moderate and similar to the body to which it is applied by nature; and because our nature is moist and hot, such drugs are often called moist and hot. The mixture of the drug that softens what is indurated, on the other hand, is much hotter than the well-mixed nature, yet not already strongly hot. For the condition of indurated parts begins from a viscous and thick flux that has become wedged in the small pores of the part. When the fine-particled moisture in it has been dispersed, and the remainder has then been chilled and has undergone a sort of congealing, what is called induration follows. For this reason they also say that the affection is cold, and that its cure is through warming agents. But since there was superfluous moisture together with the chilling, the remedy too will be compound: the condition requires warming agents because of the chilling, and evacuating agents because of the foreign and superfluous moisture.

K11.726-727K. XI 726–727, ch. 5.7, 5.8 difficulty mid (1.5/100; thin 2, absent 0) (a) pass (b) fail 2/3 (c) pass 6/6 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
ὅθεν οὐδὲ θεραπεύεται τῶν σκιῤῥουμένων οὐδὲν οὔθ’ ὑπὸ τῶν σφοδρῶς ξηραινόντων οὔθ’ ὑπὸ τῶν ἰσχυρῶς θερμαινόντων 727 οὔθ’ ὑπὸ τῶν ταῦτ’ ἄμφω δυναμένων. τὰ μὲν γὰρ σφοδρῶς θερμαίνοντα διαφορήσαντα βιαίως τὸ παρακείμενον καὶ περιεχόμενον ἐν τοῖς μορίοις ὑγρὸν, ἀποξηραίνει τὸ ὑπόλοιπον, ὡς ἀνίατον γενέσθαι· τὰ δὲ σφοδρῶς ξηραίνοντα, κᾂν μὴ θερμαίνῃ ταῦτα, οὐδὲ διὰ μέσου τοῦ κενοῦντος τὸ λεπτομερὲς, ἀλλ’ ἄντικρύς τε καὶ κατὰ τὴν ἑαυτῶν φύσιν εἰς ἄκρον ξηρότητος προσάγει τὸ ἐσφηνωμένον ἐν τοῖς σκιῤῥουμένοις ῥεῦμα. μόνα τοίνυν ὅσα θερμαίνει μὲν, οὐ μὴν σφοδρῶς, ἅμα τῷ μηδὲ ξηραίνειν ἰσχυρῶς ἰᾶται τὰς τοιαύτας διαθέσεις, καὶ καλεῖται τὰ τοιαῦτα φάρμακα μαλακτικὰ, καθ’ ἕνα καιρὸν ἄμφω δρῶντα καὶ χέοντα τὸ πεπηγὸς καὶ κατὰ βραχὺ διαφοροῦντα. Θερμὰ τοίνυν ἐστὶ καὶ οὐ πάνυ τι ξηρὰ πάντα τὰ τοιαῦτα φάρμακα, διαφέροντα τῶν ἐκπυϊσκόντων, τῷ θερμότερα ὑπάρχειν αὐτῶν καὶ ξηρότερα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Hence none of the scirrhous conditions is cured either by agents that dry vehemently, or by those that heat strongly, 727 or by those that are capable of both of these. For those that heat vehemently, having dispersed by force the moisture lying beside and contained within the parts, dry out the remainder, so that it becomes incurable; while those that dry vehemently, even if they do not heat, bring the flux wedged in the scirrhous parts to the extreme of dryness, not through the intermediate step of evacuating the fine-particled portion, but directly and in accordance with their own nature. Therefore only those which heat, yet not vehemently, together with not drying strongly, cure such conditions; and such drugs are called softening, since at one and the same time they do both things, melting what is congealed and little by little dispersing it. All such drugs, then, are hot and not very dry, differing from the pus-producing ones in that they are hotter and drier than these.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σκιῤῥουμένων, κᾂν μὴ θερμαίνῃ ταῦτα, οὐδὲ διὰ μέσου τοῦ κενοῦντος τὸ λεπτομερὲς, ἐκπυϊσκόντων
Hence none of the parts undergoing scirrhous hardening is cured either by the drugs that dry vehemently, or by those that heat strongly, 727 or by those capable of both. For the drugs that heat vehemently, having violently dispersed the moisture lying alongside and contained in the parts, dry out what remains, so that it becomes incurable. The drugs that dry vehemently, even if they do not heat, and not through the intermediate step of evacuating the fine-particled part, nevertheless bring the flux wedged in the scirrhous parts to the extreme of dryness, directly and in accordance with their own nature. Only those drugs, then, which heat, but not vehemently, and at the same time do not dry strongly either, cure such conditions. Such drugs are called softening, since they do both things at one and the same time: they liquefy what has been congealed and disperse it little by little. All such drugs, then, are hot and not very dry. They differ from the suppurative drugs in being hotter and drier than these.

Greek (Kühn)
τὰ μὲν γὰρ σύμφυλόν τε καὶ ὁμοιοτάτην ἔχει τοῖς σώμασιν ἡμῶν τὴν θερμότητα, τὰ δὲ μαλάττοντα σφοδρότερα μὲν, οὐ 728 μὴν ὥστε τῷ βιαίῳ τῆς ὁλκῆς καταξηραίνειν τὸ ὑπόλοιπον. ἔτι δὲ τὰ μὲν ἐκπυΐσκσντα τὴν προϋπάρχουσαν ἐν τοῖς σκιῤῥουμένοις μορίοις ὑγρότητα φυλάττει, τὰ δὲ μαλακτικὰ βραχύ τι μέρος αὐτῆς ἀναλίσκει. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μυρίων ὄντων τῶν θερμαινόντων καὶ ξηραινόντων φαρμάκων, ὅσα συμμέτρου τε ἅμα θερμότητος μετέχει ἀνάλογόν τε ταύτῃ ξηρότητος εὐτύχησε, ταῦτα μόνα μαλακτικὰ τῶν σκιῤῥουμένων ἐστὶν, οἷον βδέλλιον καὶ στύραξ καὶ χαλβάνη καὶ ἀμμωνιακὸν θυμίαμα καὶ μυελὸς ἐλάφειός τε καὶ μόσχειος, καὶ στέαρ αἴγειόν τε καὶ ταύρειον, ὅσα τ’ ἄλλα τοιαῦτα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐκπυΐσκσντα
For the former have a heat that is akin and most similar to that of our bodies, whereas the softening ones are more vehement, yet not 728 so much so as to dry up the remainder by the violence of their attraction. Further, the suppurative ones preserve the moisture already present in the parts affected by scirrhus, whereas the softening ones consume some small portion of it. And for this reason, though the heating and drying drugs are countless, those which partake at once of a moderate heat and have had the good fortune to possess a dryness proportionate to it—these alone are softening of parts affected by scirrhus, such as bdellium, storax, galbanum, ammoniac incense, deer's and calf's marrow, goat's and bull's fat, and whatever others are of this kind.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκπυΐσκσντα (misprint for ἐκπυΐσκοντα, rendered 'suppurating ones'), σφοδρότερα (taken as 'more intense' in heat), εὐτύχησε (rendered 'have happened to have')
For the former possess a heat that is akin and most similar to our bodies, whereas the softening ones are more intense, though not 728 so much as to dry out the remainder by the violence of their drawing. Further, the suppurating ones preserve the moisture already present in the parts undergoing skirrhos, whereas the softening ones consume some small part of it. And for this reason, though heating and drying drugs are countless, only those which share in a moderate heat and have also happened to have a dryness proportionate to it are softening of parts undergoing skirrhos: for example bdellium, storax, galbanum, ammoniac incense, the marrow of deer and of calf, the fat of goat and of bull, and all other such things.

Καὶ μέν γε ὡς ἐμπλαστικὸν εἶναι χρὴ τὸ τοιοῦτον φάρμακον, ἵν’ ἀκριβῶς ᾖ διαπυητικὸν, ἤδη μοι καὶ τοῦτο δοκῶ φαίνεσθαι σαφῶς. εἰ γὰρ τὴν οὐσίαν αὐξῆσαι. τῆς ἐμφύτου θερμότητος, οὐκ ἐπιθεῖναι προσήκει τὴν ποιότητα, πεφράχθαι χρὴ τοὺς πόρους τοῦ σώματος, ἵν’ ἔνδον ἀποστέγωσι τὰς ἀτμώδεις διαπνοάς. ὡς ὅσα γε τῶν καταπλασμάτων ἢ τῷ ῥύπτειν ἢ τῷ θερμαίνειν σφοδρότερον ἐπιτρέπει διαφορεῖσθαι τοὺς ἀτμοὺς, ξηραίνει μὲν, οὐ μὴν ἐμπυΐσκει γε.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπιθεῖναι, τί δὴ τὸ καθόλου
For at present it is not my purpose to go through all the material, but to produce one general teaching concerning all such powers—namely, what the general point is which it is better to know about both the suppurating and the softening agents: that the former kindle a heat equal to that of the animal according to nature, whereas those that soften kindle much more; and that the former by nature act not by the quality of the heat but rather by its quantity, whereas those that are softening of indurated parts 729 act rather by the quality. For just as, if it were possible to keep one's hands or some other limb constantly laid upon the part that needs suppuration, it would in this way suppurate most quickly, in the same manner, if the drug too were most similar to the mixture of the human being, it will most quickly concoct what needs to be changed into pus. And what needs this is sometimes the flesh, when it has been bruised, and sometimes the humour that produces the inflammation. And indeed, that such a drug must be clogging, in order to be precisely suppurative, this too now seems to me to appear clearly. For if it is fitting to increase the substance of the innate heat, and not to intensify (ἐπιθεῖναι) its quality, the pores of the body must be blocked, so that they may keep the vaporous exhalations shut in within; since those poultices at any rate which, either by cleansing or by heating more vehemently, allow the vapours to be dispersed, do indeed dry, but certainly do not produce pus.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τί δὴ τὸ καθόλου (perhaps to be read τὸ δὴ καθόλου; taken here as a question), ἐπιθεῖναι (possibly corrupt for ἐπιτεῖναι, 'intensify', cf. ἐπιτείνεσθαι in the following passage), αὐξῆσαι. (stray punctuation in Kühn, ignored)
For it is not our present purpose to go through all the material, but to give a single general instruction covering all such powers. And what is the general point that it is better to know about those that bring to suppuration and those that soften? That the former kindle a heat equal to the natural heat of the animal, whereas those that soften kindle a much greater one; and that the former are naturally disposed to act not by the quality of the heat but rather by its quantity, whereas those that are 729 softening of indurated parts act rather by its quality. For just as, if it were possible to keep the hands, or some other limb, always laid upon the part that needs suppuration, it would in this way come to suppuration very quickly, in the same way, if the drug too is most similar to the mixture of the human being, it will very quickly concoct what needs to be changed into pus. What needs this is sometimes the flesh, when it has been bruised, and sometimes the humour that produces the inflammation. And indeed, that such a drug must be clogging if it is to be strictly suppurative, this too, I think, is already clearly apparent. For if what is fitting is to increase the substance of the innate heat, and not to add to (or intensify) its quality, the passages of the body must be blocked, so that they may keep the vaporous exhalations shut in within. For those poultices that, either by cleansing or by heating more strongly, allow the vapours to be dispersed, do dry, but they do not bring to suppuration.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐλύμου, ταύτης
Such are the meal of barley, of chickpeas, of fenugreek and of beans, 730 and still more that of darnel, bitter vetch, Italian millet (elymos), lupines, millet, and of all drying things. For under the action of cleansing agents, even if they do not heat strongly, since the pores are unblocked and opened, the heat of the part also departs along with them, so that its natural due proportion is not preserved; and under the action of those that heat strongly, no small amount of this too is in fact evacuated, while what remains is somewhat heated, and thus it comes about that the substance of the innate heat becomes less, while its quality is intensified. But neither of these should occur; rather, the vaporous and hot pneuma should be contained in the greatest quantity, yet be precisely well-proportioned in its heat; for it is indeed this which, being present in greatest quantity in children too, helps to increase all the natural activities. There are, then, some who do not recognise even this very point, and, besides not recognising it, bring a charge against Hippocrates for saying that growing things have the greatest amount of innate heat.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐλύμου, αἰρῶν, ὑποθερμαίνεται, συναπέρχεται, ἀκριβῶς δ’ εἶναι σύμμετρον τῇ θερμότητι
Of this kind are the meal of barley and of chickpeas, and that of fenugreek and that of beans, 730 and still more that of darnel, bitter vetches, elymos, lupins, millet, and of all the drying substances. For under the action of the cleansing substances, even if they do not heat strongly, the pores are unblocked and opened, and the heat of the part departs together with what is carried off, so that its natural due proportion is not preserved. Under the action of the strongly heating substances, on the other hand, not a little of this heat is emptied out, while what remains is heated up further; and so it comes about that the substance of the innate heat becomes less, while its quality is intensified. But neither of these ought to happen. Rather, the vaporous and hot pneuma ought to be contained in the greatest amount, and it ought to be exactly proportionate in its heat. For it is this, present in the greatest amount in children, that helps increase all the natural functions. There are, then, some who do not know even this very thing, and who, besides not knowing it, find fault with Hippocrates for saying that growing bodies have the most innate heat.

ἀλλ’ ἡμᾶς χρὴ μήτε παρακούειν αὐτοῦ καὶ γιγνώσκειν ἔμφυτον εἰρῆσθαι θερμὸν, ὅπερ καὶ πνεῦμα ἑκάστῳ τῶν ζώων ὀνομάζομεν, ὑπὲρ 731 οὗ καὶ Ἀριστοτέλης ἔγραψεν. οὐδὲν δὲ κωλύει καὶ τὴν αἱματικὴν οὐσίαν καὶ ἀερώδη θερμὸν ἔμφυτον ἀκούειν ἅμα τῷ πνεύματι. οἱ μὲν οὖν Στωϊκοὶ ταὐτὸν τοῦτο τὸ πνεῦμα τὴν οὐσίαν τῆς ψυχῆς εἶναι δοξάζουσιν· ἡμεῖς δὲ περὶ οὐσίας ψυχῆς οὔτε πάνυ τι τολμῶμεν ἀποφαίνεσθαι καὶ πρὸς τὰ παρόντα περιττὸν ὑπολαμβάνομεν. ὅτι μέντοι τὸ σύμφυτον πνεῦμα, κᾂν εἰ μὴ τῆς ψυχῆς ἐστιν ἡ οὐσία, ἀλλὰ τὸ πρῶτον αὐτῆς ὄργανον ὑπάρχει, φθάνομεν ἐν τοῖς περὶ τῶν Ἱπποκράτους καὶ Πλάτωνος δογμάτων ὑπομνήμασιν ἀποδεδειχέναι. καὶ δὴ καὶ περιέχεσθαι μὲν ἐν ἅπασι τοῖς τοῦ ζώου μέλεσι τουτὶ τὸ πνεῦμά φαμεν, οὐχ ὅμοιον δ’ εἶναι πανταχόθεν, καθάπερ οὐδὲ τὸ αἷμα ἀναγκαῖον. περιέχεσθαι δὲ κᾀν ταῖς κεναῖς χώραις οὐκ ὀλίγην οὐσίαν αὐτοῦ, μᾶλλον δὲ οἷον ὕλην τινὰ βραχείας μὲν ἔτι μεταβολῆς δεομένην εἰς γένεσιν ἐμφύτου πνεύματος ἠκριβωμένου κατὰ τὰς οἰκείας ποιότητας, ἤδη μέντοι πρός γε τὰ φυσικὰ τῶν ἔργων ἱκανῶς συντελοῦσαν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μήτε παρακούειν αὐτοῦ καὶ γιγνώσκειν, κεναῖς χώραις
But we must not misunderstand him, and must recognize that 'innate heat' has been said of that which we also call pneuma in each of the animals, about 731 which Aristotle too wrote. And nothing prevents one from understanding by 'innate heat' also the blood-like and airy substance together with the pneuma. Now the Stoics hold that this very pneuma is the substance of the soul; but we neither venture at all to pronounce on the substance of the soul, and we consider it superfluous for the present purpose. That, however, the innate pneuma, even if it is not the substance of the soul, is at any rate its first instrument, we have already demonstrated in the commentaries On the Doctrines of Hippocrates and Plato. And indeed we say that this pneuma is contained in all the parts of the animal, but that it is not alike everywhere, just as it is not necessary that the blood be either. And that no small substance of it is contained also in the empty spaces, or rather, as it were, a kind of matter which still needs a slight change for the generation of innate pneuma perfected in its proper qualities, yet already contributes sufficiently, at least to the natural activities.

But we must not mishear him; we must recognise that what has been called innate heat is that which we also name pneuma in each of the animals, about 731 which Aristotle too wrote. Nothing, however, prevents us from also understanding by innate heat the sanguineous and airy substance together with the pneuma. Now the Stoics hold that this very pneuma is the substance of the soul; we, however, do not venture to make any pronouncement at all about the substance of the soul, and for present purposes we consider it superfluous. That the connate pneuma, even if it is not the substance of the soul, is nevertheless its first instrument, we have already demonstrated in the commentaries On the Doctrines of Hippocrates and Plato. Moreover, we say that this pneuma is contained in all the members of the animal, but that it is not alike everywhere, just as the blood need not be either; and that in the empty spaces too no small amount of its substance is contained, or rather a kind of material, as it were, which still needs a slight change for the generation of innate pneuma perfected in its proper qualities, but which already contributes sufficiently to the natural functions.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὤχρων, ἐλύμου
And surely one of the natural works is also the change into pus 732; for in this respect too it is a kind of concoction. One must, I think, keep the pneuma within as much as possible whenever something is to be concocted. But it is dispersed by cleansing things, such as the flour of barley and of beans; by drying things, such as Italian millet (ἔλυμος), common millet and lupines; by heating things, such as fenugreek is; and still more by things whose nature it is to heat and dry at the same time, such as darnel and bitter vetch, chickpeas and bitter vetchlings (ὦχροι). Most suitable for the generation of pus are, among things poured over as affusions, well-tempered water or water-and-oil; among things used for moistening, well-tempered oil; and among poultices, wheat flour prepared with water-and-oil and bread itself—and this too must be boiled in due measure. For what has been boiled for a very long time is somewhat more drying and suits the inflammations that are harder to concoct, whereas what has been boiled less suits those that are very hot and, as one might say, seething.

Now one of the natural works, surely, 732 is also the change into pus, for in this respect too it is a kind of concoction. And one must, I think, keep the pneuma inside as much as possible whenever it is proposed to concoct something. It is dispersed by things that cleanse, such as barley meal and bean meal; by things that dry, such as panic, millet and lupins; by things that heat, such as fenugreek; and still more by things whose nature is to heat and dry at once, such as darnel, bitter vetches, chickpeas and ochroi. This too must be boiled in due measure. For what has been boiled for a very long time is somewhat more drying and suits the inflammations that are harder to concoct, while what has been boiled less suits those that are very hot and, as one might say, seething.

734 For they are finer-particled in their substances, just as that of oxen and goats is coarser-particled and more earthy. It is therefore suitable only for boiling inflammations, strained through in one of the heating oils.

Greek (Kühn)
ὥσπερ δ’ οὗτος ἐνδεῖ τῆς συμμετρίας, ὡς πρὸς τὰς μέσας τῇ κράσει φλεγμονὰς καὶ φύσεις ἀνθρώπων τε καὶ μορίων, οὕτως ὑπερβάλλουσι βραχύ 735 τι ῥητίνη καὶ πίττα. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μιγνύμενα ταυτὶ πάντα τὰ νῦν εἰρημένα διαπυΐσκει συμμέτρως. ἀλλὰ ταῦτα μὲν ἤδη τῆς περὶ φαρμάκων συνθέσεώς ἐστι πραγματείας, ἧς ἐφάψασθαί πως ἠνάγκασεν ἡμᾶς ἡ τῆς θεωρίας ἀκολουθία. πάλιν δ’ ἐπὶ τὸ προκείμενον ἐπάνειμι. πρόκειται δὲ ἀφορίσαι τὴν καθόλου φύσιν, ὁποία τίς ἐστι, τῶν ἐκπυϊσκόντων. ἐῤῥέθη δ’ ὅτι συμμέτρως εἶναι χρὴ θερμαίνουσάν τε καὶ ὑγραίνουσαν αὐτὴν, ὅπερ ἑτέρως ἑρμηνευόμενόν ἐστι μήθ’ ὑπερβάλλειν μήτ’ ἐνδεῖν τῆς τοῦ μεταβάλλοντος αὐτὸ σώματος κράσεως, ἀλλ’ ὡς οἷόν τε μάλιστα παραπλήσιον ὑπάρχειν. τὸ δ’ ὅτι δυνάμει μὲν τοιοῦτόν ἐστι τὸ διαπυΐσκον φάρμακον, ἐνεργείᾳ δὲ γίγνεται τοιοῦτον ἐν τῷ πλησιάζειν ἡμῖν, εἴρηται πολλάκις ἔμπροσθεν, ἀναμνήσομέν τε καὶ νῦν, ὥστε μνημονεύειν αὐτοῦ διὰ παντός. ἡ μὲν δὴ κρᾶσις τοιαύτη τις ὑπάρχει τοῦ διαπυΐσκοντος, ἡ δὲ σώματος αὐτοῦ σύστασις ἐμπλαστική τίς ἐστιν. μάλιστα δὲ τοῦτο τοῖς γλίσχροις συμβέβηκεν, ὥσπερ τῷ στέατι τῷ χοιρείῳ καὶ βουτύρῳ καὶ λιβανωτῷ καὶ τῷ δι’ ἀλεύρου πυρίνου καταπλάσματι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But just as this (wax) falls short of the due measure, relative to inflammations that are intermediate in mixture and to the natures both of human beings and of parts, so resin and pitch exceed it by a little 735. And for this reason all the things just now mentioned, when mixed together, bring to suppuration in due measure. But these matters already belong to the treatise on the composition of drugs, which the sequence of the inquiry has compelled us to touch upon in some way. I return again to what lies before us. What lies before us is to define the general nature of suppurating agents, what sort it is. It was said that it must be heating and moistening in due measure, which, expressed otherwise, is neither to exceed nor to fall short of the mixture of the body that alters it, but to be as nearly similar to it as possible. And that the suppurating drug is of this kind in potentiality, but becomes such in actuality when it comes into contact with us, has been said many times before, and we shall recall it now too, so that it may be kept in mind throughout. The mixture of the suppurating agent, then, is of some such kind; the consistency of its body, however, is a clogging one. This belongs most of all to viscous things, as to pig's fat, butter, frankincense, and the poultice made from wheat flour.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς πρὸς τὰς μέσας τῇ κράσει φλεγμονὰς καὶ φύσεις ἀνθρώπων τε καὶ μορίων, τοῦ μεταβάλλοντος αὐτὸ σώματος, ἀναμνήσομέν
And just as this [wax] falls short of due proportion with respect to inflammations that are intermediate in their mixture, and to the natures both of men and of parts, so resin and pitch exceed it by a little 735. For this reason all these things just mentioned, when mixed together, bring to suppuration in due proportion. But these matters already belong to the treatise on the composition of drugs, which the sequence of the theory has in a way compelled us to touch upon. I return again to the matter proposed. What is proposed is to define the universal nature of the things that bring to suppuration, of what kind it is. It was said that this nature must heat and moisten in due proportion. Expressed otherwise, it must neither exceed nor fall short of the mixture of the body that alters it, but must be as nearly similar to it as possible. That the drug which brings to suppuration is such in potentiality, but becomes such in actuality on being brought into contact with us, has been said many times before. We shall recall it now as well, so that it may be remembered always. Such, then, is the mixture of what brings to suppuration; the consistency of its body is something clogging. This belongs above all to viscous things, such as pig's fat, butter, frankincense, and the poultice made with wheaten meal.

736 For these are moderately hot and moderately moist and viscous, and for this reason they are suitable for suppurations.
