And for this reason the scirrhous swellings too are in every case either phlegmatic 737 in their substance, or melancholic, or mixed from both. Now, it is not the occasion at present to go through their diagnosis; but it is now time for me to speak about the softening drugs, since they specifically name "softening" those that are curative of the scirrhous swellings that have arisen from dried-out phlegm and thick juice (χυλός). And since there is a not inconsiderable latitude of more and less in bodies that have been hardened, it is necessary, surely, that 738 the latitude of the drugs that cure them also be not small; so, for instance, even goat's fat has sometimes benefited bodies hardened in this way, and so has hen's fat. But these are weaker and soften only moderate hardnesses; goose fat, however, is stronger than that of hens and cocks, and he-goat's fat is stronger than that of goats, as is bull's fat, though less so than he-goat's. Moreover, deer's marrow also softens sufficiently, and after it calf's. For all such drugs are hot and moderately dry, and by nature they heat far more than they dry. Hence, if someone should at some point call them hot and moist in their powers, one must concede the point; for they stand only a little apart from things that neither dry nor moisten. Of the same genus as those just mentioned, but stronger, are ammoniac incense, storax, galbanum, and Scythian bdellium. Among these, the fresh ones are better for softening; for when they grow old they dry more strongly than they should. Marrow and fat, too, have this property.

Greek (Kühn)
740 Ἡ μὲν δὴ τῶν μαλαττόντων φαρμάκων δύναμις αὐτάρκως μοι δεδήλωται, περὶ δὲ τῶν σκληρυνόντων ἐφεξῆς δίειμι. χρὴ τοίνυν εἶναι ταῦτα ψυχρὰ καὶ ὑγρὰ, καθάπερ ἀείζωόν τέ ἐστι καὶ ἀνδράχνη καὶ ψύλλιον ὅ τ’ ἐπὶ τῶν τελμάτων φακὸς, ἢ τὸ στρύχνον, ἢ τοῦτο μὲν οὐδὲ ὑγρόν ἐστι τὴν κρᾶσιν, ἀλλὰ μέσον ὑγραίνοντος ἁπλῶς καὶ ξηραίνοντος ἐξ ἐναντίων συγκείμενον δυνάμεων, ὑγραινούσης τε καὶ ξηραινούσης, ὅτι καὶ διττῆς οὐσίας μετέχει, γεώδους τε καὶ ὑδατώδους. ἀλλ’ οὐ πρόκειταί μοι νῦν ἐπὶ τὴν κατὰ μέρος ὕλην ἀπάγειν τὸν λόγον, ἀλλὰ τὰς γενικὰς μόνον ἀφορίσασθαι δυνάμεις. εἰ μὲν δή τι καὶ ψύχει καὶ ξηραίνει, σκληρύνει μὲν καὶ τοῦτο πάντως, ἀλλ’ οὐκ ἔστι τῶν ἰδίων σκληρυντικῶν. πήξει γὰρ μᾶλλον ἢ κενώσει τὸ σκληρὸν γίγνεται σῶμα, καθότι καὶ πρόσθεν ἐλέγομεν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
740 The power of the softening drugs, then, has been sufficiently shown by me; next I shall go through the hardening ones. These, accordingly, must be cold and moist, as houseleek is, and purslane and fleawort and the lentil that grows on marshes, or strychnos—or rather this last is not even moist in its mixture, but intermediate between what simply moistens and what dries, being composed of opposite powers, a moistening and a drying one, because it also partakes of a twofold substance, earthy and watery. But it is not my purpose now to lead the discussion off into the particular materials, but only to define the generic powers. If, then, something both cools and dries, this too certainly hardens, but it does not belong to the hardening drugs properly so called. For a body becomes hard by congealing rather than by evacuation, as we also said before.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἢ τοῦτο μὲν οὐδὲ ὑγρόν ἐστι, μέσον ὑγραίνοντος ἁπλῶς καὶ ξηραίνοντος

εἴ τι δὲ ἐκ τοῦ τὴν σύμφυτον ὑγρότητα μὴ κατέχειν σκληρυνθῇ, ξηρὸν μᾶλλον τοῦτο προσαγορεύομεν ἢ σκληρὸν, καὶ ἡ ἴασις αὐτοῦ δίανσίς τε καὶ ὕγρανσίς ἐστι, καὶ οὐ μάλαξις, 741 ὥσπερ καὶ τοῦ συντεταμένου μὲν ἡ ἴασις χάλασίς ἐστι, τοῦ κεχαλασμένου δὲ σύντασις. Ταῦτα δὲ ὁρᾶται σαφῶς πῶς ἐστι καὶ ἐπὶ τοῦ δέρματος μὲν, ὥσπερ καὶ πρὸς Ἱπποκράτους εἴρηται, δέρματος σκληροῦ μάλθαξις, συντεταμένου δὲ χάλασις, οὐ μὴν ἀλλὰ κᾀπὶ τῶν ἄρθρων πολλάκις. ἡ μὲν οὖν χάλασις ὑγρανθέντων ἀμέτρως τῶν ἀμφ’ αὐτὰ συνδέσμων ἀποτελεῖται καὶ τῶν τενόντων, ἡ δὲ σύντασις οὐκέθ’ ἁπλῶς, ἀλλ’ εἴ τι ξηραινομένων ἐπιπλέον, ἢ ψυχομένων, ἢ φλεγμαινόντων, ἢ σκιῤῥουμένων. κατ’ ἄρθρα μὲν οὕτως· ἐπὶ δὲ τοῦ δέρματος οὐχ οὕτω μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῶν ἐντὸς αὐτοῦ μυῶν καθ’ ὁντιναοῦν τρόπον εἰς ὄγκον αὐξανομένων, ὥστε καὶ διὰ πολυσαρκίαν τισὶν ἐκτείνεται πολλάκις. ὅτι δὲ κᾀν ταῖς φλεγμοναῖς ταὐτὸ τοῦτο πάσχει λέλεκται πρόσθεν, ὅθεν οὔθ’ ἁπλῶς ἕν τι τῶν χαλαστικῶν ἐστιν εἶδος βοηθημάτων, ἀλλὰ τὰ μὲν ὑγραίνοντα χαλᾷ, τὰ δὲ θερμαίνοντα, τὰ δὲ μαλάττοντα, τὰ δὲ κενοῦντα, τὰ δὲ τοὺς παρὰ φύσιν ὄγκους καθαίροντα, τὰ δέ τινα τούτων κατὰ συζυγίαν ἐργαζόμενα.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: δίανσίς
But if something has become hard from not retaining its innate moisture, we call this dry rather than hard, and its cure is a thorough wetting (διανσις) and a moistening, and not a softening, 741 just as the cure of what is tensed is relaxation, and of what is relaxed, tensing. And how these things are is clearly seen both in the case of the skin, as has also been said by Hippocrates, "of hard skin, softening; of tensed, relaxation", and indeed also, often, in the case of the joints. Now relaxation is brought about when the ligaments around them and the tendons have been immoderately moistened, whereas tensing is no longer simple, but occurs whenever they are dried to excess, or chilled, or inflamed, or hardened into scirrhus. In the joints it is so; but in the case of the skin not only so, but also when the muscles within it are increased in bulk in whatever manner, so that in some people it is often stretched even on account of corpulence. And that it suffers this same thing in inflammations as well has been said before; hence there is not simply one single kind of relaxing remedies, but some relax by moistening, some by heating, some by softening, some by evacuating, some by clearing away the swellings contrary to nature, and some by effecting certain of these in combination.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: δίανσίς, εἴ τι (ξηραινομένων ἐπιπλέον), μάλθαξις, καθαίροντα
But if something has become hard from not retaining its innate moisture, we call it dry rather than hard, and its cure is soaking and moistening, not softening, 741 just as the cure of what is contracted is relaxation, and of what is relaxed, contraction. How these things stand is clearly seen in the skin, as Hippocrates too has said: 'of hard skin, mollification; of contracted skin, relaxation'. It is also often seen in the joints. Relaxation is brought about when the ligaments around them and the tendons have been moistened beyond measure. Contraction, however, does not arise in one simple way: it arises if they are dried to some greater degree, or chilled, or inflamed, or become scirrhous. So it is at the joints. In the skin it arises not only in this way but also when the muscles within it increase in bulk in any manner whatsoever, so that in some people it is often stretched even through excess of flesh. That it suffers this same thing in inflammations as well has been said before. Hence there is not simply one single kind of relaxing remedies. Rather, some relax by moistening, others by warming, others by softening, others by evacuating, others by clearing away the swellings contrary to nature, and others by effecting some of these in combination.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
742 But Thessalus and nearly all his followers, as in many other matters, so too in these confuse both the names and the things, writing down whatever occurs to them without testing it; whence it will perhaps be necessary for us also, once we have completed the matters at hand, to write at some point against them. For the present, however, let us go through what is continuous with what has been said, making our beginning once more from the drugs called clogging, since they too have been spoken of frequently. To put it briefly, then, a clogging drug is one which is plastered into the pores in the human body in a way that is hard to loosen. I have spoken sufficiently about the nature of the pores which we posit in the treatises On Mixtures. And it was demonstrated in the book before this one that the clogging drug must also be entirely non-biting. For if anything biting were present in it, it will not be able to remain in the pores, but will easily be expelled, either having melted away something of the parts, or in any case having drawn moisture from the depth.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: περιτῆξαν, ἐμπλάσσηται δυσαπολύτως
742 Thessalus, however, and nearly all his followers, as in many other matters, so in these too confound both the names and the things themselves, writing down without examination whatever comes into their heads; hence it will perhaps be necessary for us too, once we have completed the task before us, to write against them at some time. For the present, let us go through what follows on from what has been said, making a fresh start from the drugs called clogging, since they too have frequently been discussed. To put it briefly, then, a clogging drug is one that becomes plastered into the pores throughout the human body in such a way that it is hard to release. I have spoken sufficiently about the nature of the pores that we posit in my commentaries On Mixtures. And it was demonstrated in the book before this one that the clogging drug must also be wholly non-biting. For if anything biting were present in it, it will not be able to remain in the pores, but will be readily excreted, either after melting away some of the parts around it, or at any rate after drawing moisture to itself from the depth.

And it is clear 743 that the clogging drug must also be earthy in its consistency, or at any rate viscous. But about these matters enough has been said earlier too. The opposite to it would be called cleansing-out, or unblocking of the pores, just as the clogging drug itself is not only clogging but also obstructing. And it would be, just as it is opposite in its effects, so also in the nature of its body neither viscous nor non-biting, but nitrous and fine-particled. For indeed it is in the more and the less that they differ from one another, not differing in the genus of their substance—the clogging drugs from those that foster dirt, and those that clean out the pores from the cleansing ones. For those which remove the surface dirt, whether of ulcers or of the skin itself, are called cleansing; but those which also clean out the pores are both finer-particled than these and opposite to the clogging ones, and for this reason are named unblocking and purifying of the pores.

These are nitrous and bitter; and when applied externally to the skin 744 they need to possess only the nitrous quality in an exact degree in order to be able to do what has been said, but when taken into the interior of the body, even if they share in some astringency, they could even so still be purgative and thoroughly cleansing of the larger pores, such as those in the vessels are. For externally the smallness of the pores, being closed up beforehand by the astringency before they have been properly cleaned out, neither thereafter admits the cleansing substance any further into the depth nor is cleaned out. But all the parts about the belly and those about the liver and spleen and all the other viscera, having very large pores in themselves, are benefited with respect to the strength of the vessels more than they are harmed with respect to the smallness of the orifices. And for this reason wormwood is naturally fitted to clean out thoroughly from within, but not from without; and it is compounded, as was said before, of a bitter and an acerbic power. Accordingly I have no need to write out in addition the particular instances of any such drugs either.

These are nitrous and bitter. When they are applied to the skin from outside, 744 they need only possess the nitrous quality in its exact form to be able to do what has been stated. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: σέριφον
For whatever things you find to be nitrous and bitter, know that these are able to clear out all the pores; but the filth of ulcers, or indeed of the skin, not only these but 745 also those more moderate in their powers are of a nature to remove, such as the fine-particled sweet things, like honey and some of the cereal seeds, such as bitter vetch, beans, barley and lupins. And yet even among these themselves the flour of beans and of barley only cleanses, but does not unblock the pores; whereas that of bitter vetch and of lupins, and especially when they are bitter, has, in addition to cleansing, already something also clearing of the pores. Something similar also holds for almonds. For of these too, those that are bitter both cleanse and clear out the pores, whereas those that are edible cleanse but do not unblock. The seed of the nettle, however, clears the pores in the same way as bitter vetch and bitter almonds. And of the same kind, evidently, are also squill and iris, and all the others which, as has been said, have the bitter quality predominant in themselves. For nitre itself by itself, and aphronitron, and the foam of nitre, and seriphon, and southernwood, and all other such things, when taken with foods and drinks, are of the same power.

And straightaway it belongs to all of these also to be thinning of thick and viscous humours, just as it belongs to all the clogging ones 746 to make the humours in the body of the human being thick and viscous. 747 What it is still necessary for me to note in addition to what has been said will alone be stated: that whenever we wish to stimulate a more copious urination, one should not make much use of the drugs that have been mentioned, but of those that are both more pungent and at the same time more heating. For it was said earlier that everything pungent is hot. Of such a kind are the seed of celery, of rock-parsley, of fennel, of daucus, of wild celery and of smyrnion, and indeed also seseli, ammi, phou and meon, asaron and acoron; by these the blood is not only thinned but also diffused and separated, in a manner similar to milk, the serous and thin part being set apart by itself, and the thicker part in turn consolidating with itself and being precisely unified. For towards the kidneys' readily drawing to themselves whatever is watery and thin and serous in the blood, both of these contribute: first the diffusion of the whole blood itself, and following upon it the separation, none of which can come about without strong heat. And for this reason all such drugs work against the expectorations of pus from the chest.

747 What I must still point out in addition to what has been said will simply be stated: that whenever we wish to provoke a more copious urination, we should not use the drugs mentioned at all, but rather those that are both more pungent and more heating. For it was said earlier that everything pungent is hot. Such are the seed of selinon, of petroselinon, of fennel, of daucus, of wild selinon and of smyrnion, and also seseli, ammi, phu and meon, as well as asaron and acoron. By these the blood is not only thinned but also liquefied and separated, in the same way as milk: the whey-like and thin part is separated off by itself, while the thicker part condenses upon itself and is closely united. For both of these things contribute to the kidneys' easily drawing to themselves whatever in the blood is watery, thin and whey-like: first the liquefaction of the whole blood itself, and after it the separation. Neither of these can come about without strong heat. And for this reason all such things work against the expectoration of pus from the chest.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
For their nature, 748 as has just been said, is heating and drying, and moreover contracting and separating, the thick part being gathered together into one, while whatever serous and thin matter is carried in the blood is secreted and separated off in accordance with the coming-together of the former. Now this the kidneys are the first to draw to themselves, but what is being condensed and dried out is no longer easily expectorated. And for this reason, as was said just now, such a drug must be cutting, yet not conspicuously hot, so that it may not dry strongly, and it must be given together with moistening gruels and drinks for the sake of the same use. Those which cleanse the kidneys, however, are likewise cutting, but there is no need at all of abundant moisture. And those which are suited to cut the stony concretions in them are themselves also sufficiently cutting, but partake least of heat; for heat, by drying out, consolidates the stone, and does not cut and divide it.

For their 748 nature, as has just been said, is heating and drying, and besides this gathering-together and separating: the thick is gathered together into one, while whatever serous and thin matter is carried along in the blood is excreted and separated off in the course of the thick's coming together. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑποστάσεις, τυφλωθείσας
And indeed, of their opposites too, if something draws the pores together, they call it condensing; but if something closes the mouth, they no longer address it by a name of its own, 750 naming it instead by certain more generic ones: contracting, closing, constricting and stopping up. The nature of each: that of the rarefying ones is moderately hot and least of all drying and fine-particled; that of the mouth-opening ones coarse-particled, pungent and biting. Of their opposites, the condensing nature is cooling, yet neither earthy nor airy, but rather watery; while that which closes the openings is coarse-particled and cold. Examples of these: of rarefying nature are chamomile and marsh-mallow and the oil made from them, and not least also that made from wild cucumbers. But old oil too, and castor oil, and radish oil are of the same kind. Of mouth-opening power are all things that are both pungent and earthy: cyclamen, garlic, onions, the biles of bulls, the sediments of all the unguents that are at once coarse-particled and hot, such as the iris unguent and the marjoram unguent, which indeed also open up haemorrhoids that have become blind.

And moreover, as for their opposites: if something draws the pores together, they name it condensing; but if something closes the orifice, they no longer address it by a proper 750 name, and instead call it by certain more generic ones: 'drawing together', 'closing', 'binding tight' and 'stopping up'. The nature of each is as follows. That of the rarefying ones is moderately hot, drying least of all, and fine-particled; that of the ones opening the mouths of vessels is coarse-particled, pungent and biting. Of their opposites, the condensing nature is cooling, yet neither earthy nor airy but rather watery; the nature that closes the openings of the vessels is coarse-particled and cold. Also the sediments of all unguents that are at once coarse-particled and hot, such as the iris unguent and the marjoram unguent, which indeed open haemorrhoids that have become blinded.

    That of the rarefying ones is moderately hot, drying least of all, and fine-particled; that of the ones opening the mouths of vessels is coarse-particled, pungent and biting.

    Of their opposites, the condensing nature is cooling, yet neither earthy nor airy but rather watery; the nature that closes the openings of the vessels is coarse-particled and cold.
  • B · negation: added

    Examples of these: of rarefying nature are chamomile and marsh-mallow and the oil made from them, and not least also the oil made from wild cucumbers. Old oil, castor oil and radish oil are also of the same kind.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: φακοῦ τοῦ ἀπὸ τῶν τελμάτων
But those drugs said to open the mouth of the womb when it has closed because of some inflammation or dryness or scirrhus 751 would be of such a power accidentally, not primarily nor in themselves—just as neither are those which, when lips or eyelids or nostrils or pharynx or foreskin or seat or some other such mouth of an organ have closed because of inflammation, have cured the inflammation and thereby also corrected the closure. For these too would be said to be resolvers of inflammation, but by no means mouth-opening. Dioscorides errs in these matters, sometimes saying that a relaxing drug, or a softening, or a moistening, or an inflammation-resolving one, is of mouth-opening power. Such, then, are the substances of the rarefying and of the mouth-opening drugs. Of those opposite to them, the substance of the condensing ones is that of cold water and of houseleek and of purslane and of green caltrop and of fleawort and of the herb which they call mouse-ears, and of the lentil from the marshes, and, to put it simply, of all things that cool without being astringent.

But the drugs said to open the mouth of the uterus when it has closed up through some inflammation, or dryness, or scirrhus 751 would be of such a power accidentally, not primarily nor in themselves. In the same way, when the lips, eyelids, nostrils, pharynx, foreskin, anus or some other such organ with a mouth have closed up through inflammation, the drugs that cured the inflammation and so also corrected the closure are not of such a power either. For these too might be called resolvent of inflammation, but in no way opening the mouths of vessels. Dioscorides errs in these matters, sometimes saying that a drug which is relaxing, or softening, or moistening, or resolvent of inflammation is of a power that opens the mouths of vessels. Such, then, are the substances of the rarefying drugs and of those opening the mouths of vessels. Of the drugs contrary to them, the substance of the condensing ones is that of cold water, houseleek, purslane, green caltrop, fleawort, the herb which they call mouse-ear, and the lentil from the pools; and, to put it simply, of whatever cools without being astringent.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: χωρὶς ἐπὶ μίκτον δριμύτητος
Hence also mandrake and hemlock, henbane and poppy—I mean now the herbs themselves—if one uses them moderately, are condensing 752 in their powers; if more, they are not only condensing but already also numbing; and if to the greatest degree, they are no longer only numbing but already also mortifying. The substance of the drugs contrary to the opening ones, being coarse-particled and cold, belongs to all astringents, without any admixture (ἐπὶ μίκτον) of pungency. Sufficient examples of their material have been given in the fourth book, in which I have also explained the substance of such drugs, which is earthy and cold. So it is no longer any wonder if it alone is of a nature to draw together and close the mouths of the vessels that have been opened up contrary to nature; for in it alone are present all the things that what is to be drawn together requires: on account of the coarse-particled character of its consistency it falls upon the parts from outside and is unable to pass through the fine pores; on account of its cooling it compresses inward and draws together the neighbouring parts from every side into themselves; and because it dries—for every astringent was shown to be such—it feeds upon the moisture and tones the part.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπὶ μίκτον (probably to be read ἐπιμίκτου, 'admixed'), νεκρωτικαί, εἰς ἑαυτὰ, πιλούσῃ, ἐκβοσκουμένη
For this reason mandrake and hemlock, henbane and poppy too (I mean now the herbs themselves), if one uses them moderately, are condensing 752 in their powers; if more liberally, not only condensing but by now numbing as well; and if most liberally, no longer only numbing but by now also deadening. The substance of the things contrary to the anastomotic [drugs], being coarse-particled and cold, is that of all the astringents that are without admixed pungency. Sufficient examples of their material have been given in the fourth book, in which I also explained the substance of such drugs as being earthy and cold. It is therefore no longer anything to wonder at if this substance alone is naturally able to draw together and close the mouths of the vessels that have opened contrary to nature. For to it alone is present everything that the things to be drawn together require. Because of the coarse-particled character of its consistency it falls upon them from outside but is unable to pass through the fine passages. Because of its cooling it compresses inwards and draws together from every side towards themselves the parts that lie near. And because it dries (for every astringent was shown to be of this kind), it consumes the moisture and gives tone to the part.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στεγνωτικὰ, στεγνόν, τὸ στεγνωτικὸν σῶμα
But if all these things come together, the orifice will be closed, being drawn together from outside by the particles of the astringent substance as if by certain fingers 753. But those drugs which are cold, either equally or more so, yet watery in their substances—such drugs both draw together and constrict only weakly, because of their softness. For everything that is going to compress and draw together anything whatever strongly requires a certain resistant and hard strength; and since the drugs that are more watery in their substances do not possess this, they draw together and condense the fine pores in each body, but are unable to constrict the whole organ from every side, so that with good reason such drugs are condensing but are not stopping. And understand me now as calling 'stopping' all those that hold back the perceptible excretions. For indeed the stopping body—that which holds within itself and lets nothing out perceptibly—has been termed by the Greeks stegnon (stopped-up).

But if all these things come together, the orifice will be closed, drawn together from outside by the particles of the astringent substance as if by fingers 753. Those drugs, however, which are cold, either to the same degree or more, but watery in their substances, both draw together and constrict weakly, because of their softness. For everything that is to compress and draw together anything strongly needs a certain resistant and hard strength; and the drugs more watery in their substances, not having this, draw together and condense the fine pores in each body, but are unable to constrict the whole organ from every side, so that such drugs are reasonably condensing, but not binding (stegnotic). Understand me now as calling 'binding' those that check the perceptible excretions. For the binding body, the one that holds in (stegon) within itself and lets nothing go out perceptibly, has been called by the Greeks stegnon, 'tight'.
Flags (places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors): none raised.

Greek (Kühn)

Ὅσα τοίνυν ἀλύπως θερμαίνει, ταῦτα ἀραιωτικὰ μόνον ὑπάρχει τῶν πάντων· εἰ δὲ μὴ θερμὰ μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ παχυμερῆ ταῖς συστάσεσιν εἴη, σφοδρὰ μὲν ὑπάρχοντα καὶ καυστικὰ συντήκει τε τὰ σώματα δίκην πυρὸς, ἐσχάρας τε πολλάκις ὁμοίας ταῖς ἀπὸ τῶν καυστήρων ἐργάζεται. ἧττον δ’ ἢ ὥστε καίειν θερμαίνοντα τῆς ἀναστομωτικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως, ὥστ’ εἶναι τῶν ἀναστομωτικὸν φάρμακον τῇ μὲν οὐσίᾳ γεῶδές τε ἅμα καὶ πυρῶδες, εἰς τοσοῦτον δ’ ἧκον θερμότητος ὡς μήπω καίειν. εἰ δὲ καυστικόν τε ἅμα καὶ μὴ μέντοι σφοδρῶς εἴη, καὶ προσέτι λεπτομερὲς, ἤτοι παντάπασιν ἄδηκτον ἔσται τὸ τοιοῦτον, ἢ μετ’ ὀλίγης δήξεως καὶ ὀδύνης ἀποτήξει τι τῶν σαρκωδῶν μορίων.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τῶν ἀναστομωτικὸν φάρμακον
These, then, are of such a kind in their mixtures and powers; but the rarefying ones heat in every case, for otherwise they could not at once diffuse and relax the substance, upon which follows also the widening of the pores. It is necessary, 754 however, that they be neither excessively hot in their mixtures — for such are by that point pungent and compel shivering — nor drying; for these too are both colliquative and painful to the sensitive bodies. Those, therefore, which heat without distress are the only ones of all that are rarefying. But if they are not only hot but also coarse-particled in their consistencies, then, when they are vehement and caustic, they melt down the bodies in the manner of fire and often produce eschars similar to those from cauteries; but when they heat less than so as to burn, they belong to the mouth-opening power, so that the mouth-opening drug (reading the transmitted τῶν as uncertain) is in its substance at once earthy and fiery, but has arrived at such a degree of heat as not yet to burn. But if it should be caustic and yet not vehemently so, and moreover fine-particled, such a thing will either be altogether non-biting, or with little biting and pain will melt away some of the fleshy parts.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τῶν (in ὥστ’ εἶναι τῶν ἀναστομωτικὸν φάρμακον, probably corrupt for τὸ), φρίττειν, εἰ δὲ καυστικόν τε ἅμα καὶ μὴ μέντοι σφοδρῶς εἴη
These, then, are of this kind in their mixtures and in their powers. Rarefying drugs, on the other hand, in every case heat; for otherwise they could not at once diffuse and relax the substance, upon which there follows also the widening of the pores. 754 They must, however, be neither excessively hot in their mixtures (for such drugs are by then pungent and compel shivering) nor drying; for these too are colliquative and painful to the sentient bodies. Those, therefore, which heat without causing distress are, of all things, the only ones that are rarefying. But if they should be not only hot but also coarse-particled in their consistencies, then, being vehement and caustic, they melt down the bodies after the manner of fire, and often produce eschars like those from cauteries. Those that heat less than is required to burn belong to the power of opening the mouths of vessels, so that the drug that opens the mouths of vessels is in its substance at once earthy and fiery, but has come to such a degree of heat as not yet to burn. If, however, it should be caustic, yet not vehemently so, and moreover fine-particled, such a drug will either be entirely free from biting, or, with slight biting and pain, will melt away some part of the fleshy parts.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συμπτώματος, οὔτε ἐσχάραν ἐργάζεται καὶ καλεῖται σηπτικὰ
For, by neither altering all at once, as the strongly hot things do, nor passing through with difficulty, as the coarse-particled things do, 755 it has an activity that escapes notice, if indeed, among alterations, it is the sudden changes that are most perceptible, and, among passages, the violent ones. For the coarse-particled caustic, in whatever part it settles, causes distress, being fixed in it like a splinter. But that kind is eschar-producing, in the same way as the caustics; whereas the drugs about which the argument is now concerned produce no eschar, and are called putrefying, though they have not obtained a name that is properly their own. For the truly putrefying things are those that moisten together with heating. Nevertheless we call them so because of the similarity of the symptom, for a painless destruction is brought about by both. Now the modes in which things are destroyed are several: for things that have been excessively cooled, excessively heated, excessively moistened and excessively dried are destroyed; but we do not say that all things that are destroyed putrefy, but only those that undergo this together with a foul smell. About the names, then, one should not be greatly concerned.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οὔτε ἐσχάραν ἐργάζεται καὶ καλεῖται (οὔτε … καί construction; text possibly corrupt), ἐνιζήσῃ, σκόλοπος
For because it neither alters all at once, as the intensely hot things do, nor passes through with difficulty, as the coarse-particled things do, 755 it has an activity that goes unnoticed, since among alterations it is the sudden changes that are most perceptible, and among passages through it is the forcible ones. For the coarse-particled caustic, in whatever part it settles, distresses it, being stuck in like a splinter. But that one is eschar-forming, in the same way as the caustics. The things with which the present discussion is concerned neither produce an eschar and [yet] are called putrefying, though they have not obtained an appropriate designation. For the truly putrefying things are those that moisten together with heating. Nevertheless we do call them so on account of the similarity of the symptom, for a painless destruction comes about from both. There are, to be sure, several modes of things being destroyed: for things that have been excessively chilled, excessively heated, excessively moistened and excessively dried are all destroyed; but we do not say that all things that are destroyed putrefy, only those that undergo this with a foul smell. About names, however, one need not be very earnest.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τῆς Ἀσίας πέτρας ἄνθος
But one must know that the drugs called septic and putrefying, such as arsenic and sandarach and chrysocolla, 756 and dryopteris and pine-caterpillar and aconite, all melt and melt away, and especially the soft flesh, without pain. Some of them they specifically name 'reducing' (kathairetika), and they use them for the scarring-over of ulcers that have excess flesh. These too are of the same power, in genus, as the one named putrefying, but weaker to such a degree that they reduce only the surface of that upon which they fall, and are unable to advance into the depth, like also the flower of the Asian stone; such things, however, are certainly not of the same power as the truly scarring one. For those are of such a nature as neither to reduce nor to melt away anything of the flesh, but to harden and to dry: alum, and the gall-nut which they call omphakitis, and burnt copper, and especially the washed. For the unwashed has something reducing in it, as does copper scale too; but the washed is the finest of the scarring drugs. For that which is going to produce a scar well must both be moderately astringent and dry.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σηπτὰ, καθαιρετικὰ, ἀρσενικὸν, σανδαράκη, τῆς Ἀσίας πέτρας ἄνθος, ὀμφακῖτιν
One must know that the drugs called putrefactive and putrefying, such as arsenikon, sandarake and chrysocolla, 756 dryopteris, the pine caterpillar and aconite, all melt and colliquate, and above all soft flesh, without pain. Some of them are specifically called reducing (kathairetika), and people use them for the scarring over of ulcers with excess flesh. These too are of the same power in genus as the one named putrefying, but weaker to such a degree that they reduce only the surface of whatever they come into contact with and are unable to penetrate into depth, as is also the case with the flower of Asian stone. Such things, however, are not of the same power as the truly scarring one. For those do not reduce or melt away any of the flesh; they are by nature apt to harden and to dry. Such are alum, the oak-gall which they call omphakitis, and burnt copper, especially when it has been washed. For the unwashed has something reducing about it, as does the scale of copper, whereas the washed is the finest of the scarring drugs. For what is going to scar well must be both moderately astringent and drying.

Συγκέχυται δὲ παρὰ τοῖς πολλοῖς τῶν ἰατρῶν οὐχ ἡ προσηγορία μόνον, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἡ τῆς δυνάμεως γνῶσις ἁπάντων τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων. ἐπουλω τικὰ γὰρ ὀνομάζουσιν ἐνίοτε καὶ τὰ καθαιρετικὰ καὶ συντηκτικὰ τῆς σαρκὸς, ὅτι χρωμένων ἡμῶν καὶ τούτοις πολλάκις εἰς οὐλὴν ἀφικνεῖται τὰ ἕλκη, ἀλλ’ οὔτε πρώτως οὔτε κατὰ τὴν οἰκείαν αὑτῶν δύναμιν, οὔτ’ ἐπὶ πάσῃ χρήσει τοῦτο γίνεται. χνοώδη γὰρ ἀκριβῶς τὰ τοιαῦτα φάρμακα διὰ τοῦ τῆς μύλης πυρῆνος ἐπικυλιομένου ταῖς σαρξὶν εἰς οὐλὴν ἄγει τὰ ἕλκη. εἰ δὲ βραχεῖ πλείονι χρήσαιο, καὶ δάκνει καὶ συντήκει τὴν σάρκα καὶ κοῖλον ἐργάζεται τὸ ἕλκος ἐναντίον τοῖς ὄντως ἐπουλωτικοῖς. εἰ μὴ γὰρ ἀξιόλογον ἐκείνων ἐπιθείης, οὐδὲν ἀνύσει. πρόκειται μὲν γὰρ ἐν ταῖς τῶν ἰσοπέδων ἑλκῶν ἐπουλώσεσιν ἀλλοιῶσαί τε τὴν σάρκα καὶ δέρμα ποιῆσαι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μύλης, πυρῆνος, ἐπικυλιομένου
757 Hence also the fruit of the Egyptian thorn, and the dry rinds of the pomegranate, and all such things are among the scarring drugs. But among the majority of physicians not only the appellation but also the knowledge of the power of all such drugs has been confused. For they sometimes name 'scarring' also the drugs that reduce and are colliquative of the flesh, because when we use these too the ulcers often arrive at a scar; but this happens neither primarily nor in accordance with their own proper power, nor with every use. For such drugs, when precisely dust-like, with the pestle of the mortar being rolled over the flesh, bring the ulcers to a scar. But if you should use a little more, it both bites and colliquates the flesh and makes the ulcer hollow, the opposite of the truly scarring drugs. For unless you apply a considerable amount of those, it will accomplish nothing. For in the scarring-over of level ulcers the task set before us is to alter the flesh and to make it skin.

757 Hence the fruit of the Egyptian thorn, the dry rinds of the pomegranate, and everything of this kind also belong among the scarring drugs. Most physicians have confused not only the name but also the knowledge of the power of all such drugs. For they sometimes also call scarring those drugs that are catheretic and colliquative of the flesh, because ulcers often come to a scar when we use these as well. But this happens neither primarily, nor by their own proper power, nor with every use. For such drugs, made exactly like fine dust and applied by means of the knob of the probe (μύλης, apparently for μήλης) rolled over the flesh, bring ulcers to a scar. But if you use a little more, they both bite and colliquate the flesh and make the ulcer hollow, the opposite of the truly scarring drugs. With those, unless you apply a considerable amount, it will accomplish nothing. For in the scarring of level ulcers the aim is to alter the flesh and make it skin.

This comes about through those [drugs] that are able to contract it and bind it 758 and compact and condense and dry and callus it; for the skin is, as it were, a kind of callused flesh. The drugs able to act in this way, then, are called scarring drugs primarily and properly; the others, the reducing ones, secondarily and accidentally, as also all those that dry without biting and without being astringent. For these too bring [wounds] to a scar accidentally, such a drug as myrrh is, and litharge; and oyster-shell, if it be burnt, and diphryges—for these too, sprinkled on dry, have often formed a scar. One must know, both in these discussions and in all the others, that where I add the word "often", I wish to indicate this: that such [drugs] sometimes also fail of what they promise, because they do not produce it primarily nor in accordance with their own power. The scarring drugs, however, are able in some measure to bring to a scar every level wound, just as the moderately and non-bitingly cleansing drugs are fillers of hollow ones.

This is brought about by those [drugs] that are able to draw it together, constrict 758 and compact it, condense and dry it, and make it callous. For skin is, as it were, a kind of callused flesh. Drugs able to act in this way are therefore called scarring primarily and in the strict sense; the others, the reducing ones, are so called secondarily and accidentally, as are also those that dry without biting and without being astringent. For these too bring [ulcers] to a scar accidentally; such a drug is myrrh, and also litharge. And if oyster-shell and diphryges are burnt, then these too, sprinkled on dry, have often scarred [ulcers] over. One must understand, both in these discussions and in all the others, that wherever I add 'often' I mean to indicate that such drugs sometimes fail to deliver what they promise, because they do not bring it about primarily, nor by their own power. Scarring drugs, however, are able to scar over every level ulcer, just as drugs that cleanse moderately and without biting are able to fill up hollow ones.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐν αὐταῖς
But there will be a very full discussion concerning the power of such things in 759 the writings on the Therapeutic Method, with which this book has shared in many places, as was also stated earlier at its beginning. Therefore I think that, having now brought to a close the account concerning these, I shall pass to another kind of powers, those named drawing and repelling. Drawing, then, are all those that attract more vehemently the things lying in the depth; repelling, all those that drive away toward the depth the humours that approach in them. The substance of the former is hot and fine-particled, that of the latter, conversely, cold and coarse-particled. For the hot always draws, and the cold repels; but the fine-particled hot draws more vehemently, and the coarse-particled cold, such as the astringent, pushes more violently. So it is from the vehemence of the activity that each has its name.

The power of such drugs will be discussed at greatest length in 759 the books of the Therapeutic Method, with which this book shares common ground in many places, as was also stated earlier at its beginning. For this reason I think that I shall now bring the discussion of these to a close and pass on to another class of powers, those named drawing and repelling. Drawing powers, then, are those that draw up more vehemently what lies in the depth; repelling powers are those that drive toward the depth the humours that come close to them. The substance of the former is hot and fine-particled, that of the latter, conversely, cold and coarse-particled. For the hot always draws, and the cold repels. But the fine-particled hot draws more vehemently, and the coarse-particled cold, such as the astringent, thrusts more forcibly. Each, then, takes its name from the vehemence of its activity.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἅπασι μὲν ἂν οὕτω γε, ἀφ’ ἑκατέρας ταύτης, ψωρικοῦ, ἀλεξητηρίων
The matter of the astringents, then, is evident; but as for that of the drawing drugs, one kind is self-grown, while another has its origin from a putrefaction that turns sour: self-grown is that of dittany, and that of propolis and thapsia, and that of sagapenum, and that of the juices, 760 the Cyrenaic and the Median, and whatever other there may be that is similar to these. From putrefaction, on the other hand, is that of leaven and that of the so-called psoricum. And now dung too, being from putrefaction—all kinds would thus indeed be of drawing power, but the difference among them is not small. For that of the pigeon is sufficiently drawing, and at an equal distance from this on either side stand that of the goose, toward the hotter, and that of the cock, toward the colder. That of humans and of pigs falls still further short of this. That of dogs is like the cleansing drugs, and more so when they eat bones. And that of the land crocodiles achieves this still more precisely. There is also another kind of drawing drugs, which attracts by affinity of quality, which is nothing other than by similarity of the whole substance, just as also things that are nourished draw to themselves their proper nourishment. Among such drugs are all the purgatives and some of the protective ones. And all such drugs must be hot.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἤδη δὲ καὶ ἡ κόπρος ἡ ἐκ σηπεδόνος ἅπασι μὲν ἂν οὕτω γε τῆς ἑλκτικῆς εἶεν δυνάμεως (syntax apparently corrupt: singular κόπρος with plural εἶεν; ἅπασι perhaps for ἅπασαι), ψωρικοῦ (the drug called psoricum; identity not certain), ὀξυνούσης (putrefaction that turns acid or sharp), ἀκριβοῖ (achieves this more exactly / more perfectly), ὀπῶν τοῦ Κυρηναίου τε καὶ Μηδικοῦ (silphium supplied)
The material of astringent [drugs] is obvious. Of the drawing [drugs], however, some material is natural (self-grown), while some has its origin from a putrefaction that turns things acid. Natural is that of dittany, of propolis and thapsia, of sagapenum, and of the juices 760 of the Cyrenaic and the Median [silphium], and of anything else, if there is any, similar to these. From putrefaction come that of leaven and that of what is called psoricum. Further, there is also dung, which arises from putrefaction. All [kinds of dung] would in this way at least be of the drawing power, but the difference among them is not small. For that of the pigeon is sufficiently drawing; and equally distant from it on either side stand that of the goose, toward the hotter, and that of the cock, toward the colder. Falling still further short of this [i.e. the cock's] are that of human beings and that of pigs. That of dogs is like the cleansing drugs, and the more so when they eat bones. And that of land crocodiles achieves this still more exactly. There is also another kind of drawing drugs, which attract by affinity of quality, which is nothing other than by similarity of the whole substance, just as things that are nourished also attract their proper nourishment. To such drugs belong all the purgative ones and some of the protective remedies. All such [drugs] must be hot.

Ἀλλ’ ἐπεὶ καὶ περὶ τούτων αὐτάρκως εἴρηται, μεταβῶμεν ἐπὶ τὰς ἀλεξητηρίους τε καὶ ἀλεξιφαρμάκους ὀνομαζομένας δυνάμεις. ἔστι δὲ καὶ τούτων ἡ φύσις διττὴ, τῶν μὲν ἀλλοιούντων, τῶν δ’ ἐκκενούντων τοῦ πεπονθότος σώματος ἢ τὸν φθαρτικὸν ἰὸν ἢ τὸ δηλητήριον φάρμακον. ἀλλοιοῖ μὲν οὖν ἤτοι κατά τινα ποιότητα μίαν ἢ συζυγίαν ἐκ δυοῖν ἢ κατὰ τὰς ὅλας οὐσίας, ἐκκενοῖ δὲ τῇ τῆς ὅλης οὐσίας ὁμοιότητι καὶ τῇ λεπτομερεῖ θερμότητι. τέτταρες οὖν αἱ πᾶσαι διαφοραὶ γενήσονται τῆς ἐξ αὐτῶν ὠφελείας, δύο μὲν ἀλλοιωτικαὶ καὶ δύο κενωτικαί. καὶ ἡ μὲν δὴ τῇ τῆς ποιότητος ἐναντιώσει βοηθοῦσα πρόδηλος.

For among things that are similar in their substances, the hotter is more drawing, 761 inasmuch as it has taken on heat as an ally to the similarity. For such a thing, drawing by two causes, will have the advantage over that which attracts by only one of them. And it will make no difference whether one speaks of an attractive (epispastikē), a drawing (helktikē) or a drawing-in (helkystikē) power. But since enough has been said about these too, let us pass on to the powers named alexeterial and alexipharmic. The nature of these too is twofold: some alter, while others evacuate from the affected body either the destructive venom or the deleterious drug. Now it alters either in respect of some single quality, or a conjugation of two, or in respect of the whole substances; and it evacuates by similarity of whole substance and by fine-particled heat. So the differences of the benefit derived from them will be four in all, two alterative and two evacuative. And the one that helps by the opposition of quality is obvious.

For among things similar in their substances, the hotter is the more drawing 761, having, as it were, taken on heat as an ally to the similarity. For such a thing, drawing by two causes, will have the advantage over that which attracts by one of them alone. It will make no difference whether one speaks of an attractive, a drawing, or a tractive power. But since enough has been said about these too, let us pass on to the powers named protective and antidotal. The nature of these too is twofold: some alter, others evacuate from the affected body either the destructive venom or the deleterious drug. They alter, then, either by some single quality, or by a pairing of two, or by their whole substances; they evacuate by the similarity of the whole substance and by fine-particled heat. In all, therefore, there will be four differences of the benefit coming from them, two alterative and two evacuative. And the one that helps by the opposition of the quality is plain.

For if the deleterious drug, or the venom of the animal, should be cold, they will be helped by heating drugs; 762 but if hot, by cooling ones; and if dry, by moistening ones, but if moist, by drying ones; and so too, if it is at once cold and moist, by drying and heating ones, but if hot and dry, by moistening and cooling ones, and analogously in the remaining two conjugations. Nor is the alteration brought about through the powers that act according to the whole substance unclear to those who remember what has been demonstrated before in the commentaries On the Natural Faculties and, in addition, in those On Mixtures. For the powers that alter the deleterious drugs are in their nature midway between the bodies that are affected and the drugs that injure them, so that there is a proportion: as the body is to the alexipharmic power, so is that power to the deleterious drug; and likewise, as the deleterious is to the alexipharmic, so too is the alexipharmic to the body. And for this reason nearly all the things that are opposed to deleterious drugs, when taken in larger quantity, greatly injure the body of the animal.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
It is necessary, therefore, that all such 763 powers be given in such a due proportion of quantity that they neither harm the body by their abundance nor, through smallness of amount, are overcome by the deleterious things. But this already belongs to the therapeutic method; for now let us hold to what comes next in order. Every deleterious venom is evacuated by drugs applied from without, such as those that effect the attraction either by heat or by the similarity of the whole substance. Here too the alexipharmic must be, as far as is at all possible, intermediate in its nature between the body which it treats and the venom which does the harm. For if it is most opposite to the body, it will rather act upon it like some deleterious thing, and will not evacuate the venom. For, to take up the whole matter once again for the sake of clarity, one must know that all deleterious powers are most opposite in mixture to the bodies harmed by them. If, then, they are evacuated by drugs similar in mixture, these too would surely be opposite to the natures of bodies.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπαμφοτερίζειν
And indeed they are in a way contrary, yet not to such a degree as to destroy, but rather they are of a double character, being established in the middle between those things that outright harm the body of the animal and 764 those that benefit it. Whether you wish to call them deleterious powers, or destructive, or corruption-producing, or anything else whatsoever, it will make no difference; and in the same way whether (you call the others) protective or alexipharmic. Nor indeed, if someone should wish to name those that are curative of venomous beasts 'protective', and those that are curative of deleterious drugs 'alexipharmic', as some have already thought fit, should one dispute with these people either over the names. For curing diseases in the appropriate manner comes not from these, but from the knowledge of the things themselves. Since about these matters too as much has been said as is proportionate to the present purpose, let us now pass on in order to the account of the pain-relieving and soothing or mitigating powers, or however one might wish to name them. A pain-relieving power, then, is in one case really such, in another only so called, as if someone were to say that even a dead man is free of pain. The really pain-relieving power, then, is that of the things that heat in the first order, whenever it is contained in a fine-particled substance.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But that of the things called cold [is anodyne only in name], when, employing an unmixed cooling, they numb 765 the part; and numbness is all but insensibility. Those things, however, which stop pains by curing the condition would not reasonably be called anodyne; for this belongs in common to all things that treat. The truly anodyne drugs, then, must be fine-particled and not much hotter than the moderate ones, for the sake of evacuating, dispersing, rarefying, thinning, concocting and evening out everything that, in the pained parts, has been caught and shut in—whether of pungent or viscous or thick humours, or of many such blocked up in fine pores, or indeed of some vaporous and thick, or sufficiently cold, pneuma—having no suitable way out.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐναπείληπται, ὁμαλῦναι, ἐμπεφραγμένων
The other is that of the so-called cold drugs, when, employing unmixed cooling, they numb 765 the part; and numbness is very nearly an absence of sensation. Those drugs that stop pains by healing the condition could not reasonably be called anodyne, for this belongs in common to all things that treat. Truly anodyne drugs, then, must be fine-particled and not much hotter than the moderate ones, so as to evacuate, disperse, rarefy, thin, concoct and even out whatever has been caught and shut up within the parts in pain: pungent, viscous or thick juices; many juices obstructed in narrow pores; or also some vaporous and thick, or considerably cold, pneuma that has no suitable outlet.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: καταφορὰν
So they must have no astringent power whatsoever attached to them, not even if the place or the affection should additionally require it; from which it is clear that sometimes [such a drug] will not benefit the condition at all but will only soothe the pain, in which case indeed it will truly be called pain-relieving, while sometimes it will also be of some benefit to the condition itself and will have a twofold power, being a drug at once pain-relieving 766 and curative. But about such matters too more will be said in the books of the Therapeutic Method. For the present it suffices to go through the point that, of these drugs specifically named pain-relieving, some, as has been said, belong to the first order of heating drugs—and these are indeed truly pain-relieving, like dill oil—while some are nearly the same [in mixture] as our own body, like those called suppuratives, about whose material it has been spoken earlier. Distinctions concerning their use will be drawn in the books of the Therapeutic Method. But those which, when drunk, produce lethargic sleep (καταφορά), and which for this reason are called by most people pain-relieving and soporific, are of the mixture most opposite to the truly pain-relieving ones. For they all cool the body and numb the sensation to such a degree that, if a little more should be drunk, they bring on death. The best of them for the use at hand are those that dry.

Greek (Kühn)
καὶ ὅσα γε πλῆθος ὑγρότητος ἔχει ψυχρᾶς, ὥσπερ καὶ τὸ κώνειον, οὐκ ἀγαθὰ πινόμενα· τοιοῦτος δ’ ἐστὶν καὶ ὁ μανδραγόρας, 767 πλὴν τοῦ φλοιοῦ τῆς ῥίζης ξηροῦ, καὶ ὑοσκύαμος, πλὴν τοῦ σπέρματος. ἄμεινον δὲ ἐν τούτοις τὸ λευκὸν σπέρμα τοῦ μέλανος. ἔνια δὲ ὅλαις ταῖς οὐσίαις ἐστὶν ἡμῖν ἐναντία, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο, κᾂν ἐλάχιστα ληφθῇ, βλάπτει πάντως, οἷον ἥ τε δρυόπτερις καὶ ἡ πιτυοκάμπη καὶ ἡ θαψία καὶ τὸ στρύχνον τὸ μανικὸν καὶ ὑδράργυρος, ἔνιοί τε τῶν μυκήτων, καὶ τὸ σίαλον καὶ χολαὶ τῶν ἰοβόλων ζώων. τὰ γὰρ τοιαῦτα πάντα τῷ γένει δηλητήρια καθέστηκεν, οὐ τῷ ποσῷ. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο οὐδὲν ἐξ αὐτῶν εἰς τὰς ἀλεξητηρίους ἀντιδότους ἐμβάλλεται, καθάπερ ὁ τοῦ μήκωνος ὀπὸς καὶ ὁ σμύρνα καὶ ὁ στύραξ καὶ ὁ κρόκος. ταῦτα γὰρ, εἰ μὲν πλείω ποθείη, τὰ μὲν ἐκμαίνει, τὰ δὲ θάνατον ἐπιφέρει. μετὰ συμμετρίας δέ τινος ἐπιμιγνύμενα τοῖς ἄλλοις ἀρήγει. ὅσα δ’ ἐξ αὐτῶν βλάπτει τὴν διάνοιαν, εὐθὺς μέν ἐστιν τὰ πλεῖστα καρηβαρικὰ, πλήθους ἀτμῶν μοχθηρῶν ἐμπιπλῶντα τὴν κεφαλήν. ἅπτεται δ’ ἔνια καὶ τοῦ στόματος τῆς γαστρὸς, ὡς συμπάσχειν τὰ κατὰ τὴν κεφαλήν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὁ σμύρνα, δρυόπτερις
And those which contain an abundance of cold moisture, as hemlock does, are not good when drunk; such too is mandrake, 767 except for the dry bark of its root, and henbane, except for its seed. Among these the white seed is better than the black. Some, however, are contrary to us in their whole substances, and for this reason, even if the smallest amount is taken, they harm in every case, such as dryopteris and the pine-caterpillar and thapsia and the mad strychnos and quicksilver, and some of the mushrooms, and the saliva and the biles of venomous animals. For all such things are deleterious by their kind, not by their quantity. And for this reason none of them is put into the protective antidotes, as are the juice of the poppy and myrrh and storax and saffron. For these, if they are drunk in too large a quantity, some drive one mad and others bring on death; but when mixed with the other ingredients in a certain due proportion they give help. Those of them which harm the understanding are, straightaway, for the most part productive of heaviness of the head, filling the head with a multitude of bad vapours. Some also attack the mouth of the stomach, so that the parts about the head are affected in sympathy.

And those that contain a quantity of cold moisture, as hemlock does, are not good when drunk. Such too is mandrake, 767 except for the dry bark of its root, and henbane, except for its seed; and among these the white seed is better than the black. Some also lay hold of the mouth of the stomach, so that the parts in the head suffer in sympathy.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἤτοι διὰ ῥινῶν καὶ στόματος γιγνομένη
To take an example at once: everyone speaks of a certain purgative power, yet not everyone grasps alike that the name signifies two things: one, something common to all things that in any way whatever clear out the superfluities of the animal; the other, something in the pre-eminent sense, applied to those which do so through vomitings or through evacuation of the belly; nor [do they grasp] that the purging which takes place either through the nostrils and 769 the mouth is of the same genus as such purgations. They call the drugs poured in through the nostrils for the sake of purging the head errhines, while all those that are gargled and chewed they gather under one name and call apophlegmatizing, and they name their powers apophlegmatic. Of the same genus as these are also all those which, when applied to the womb, purge. For all those which I have listed as purging possess a drawing power, some of some one humour, others of two or even more, and this is common to them. Whereas all those which, by thinning the thick and viscous humours, are naturally fitted either to move urine or to assist the expectorations from the chest and lung do indeed clear out as well, but are not named in the same way as the aforementioned; rather, some are called diuretic and cough-drugs, others promoters of the menses, differing from the aforementioned in that they do not possess a drawing power of humours proper to them, as those do. Accordingly, in these cases too the account requires a further distinction, of the following sort.

For instance, everyone names a certain purgative power, but not all of them equally grasp that the name signifies two things: the one a common sense, applied to all things that in any way whatever clear out the residues of the animal; the other a sense by pre-eminence, applied to those that do so through vomitings or the drawing down of the belly. Nor do they grasp that the [purgation] which takes place through the nostrils and 769 mouth is of the same kind as such purgations. The drugs that are poured in through the nostrils for the sake of purging the head they call errhines; those that are gargled and those that are chewed they take together under one name and call apophlegmatizing, and they name their powers apophlegmatic. Of the same kind as these are also those that purge when applied to the uterus. For all the things that I have listed as purging have a drawing power, some of a single humour, others of two or even more, and this is common to them. For those that are of a nature, by thinning the thick and viscous humours, to provoke urine or to assist the expectorations from the thorax and lung, clear out as well, but they are not named in the same way as the aforementioned. Rather, some are called diuretic and cough drugs, others drugs that bring on the menses. They differ from the aforementioned in that they do not, as those do, have a drawing power of humours proper to them. In these cases too, however, the account needs a further distinction, as follows.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Those which are applied to the womb in pessaries, or fomentations, or in some such manner, 770 act by a twofold cause: some provoking [the flow] by heating alone, others by the so-called drawing and purgative powers, which we have shown to produce the evacuations by affinity with the things drawn. But those which are drunk produce the evacuations by thinning the blood and by unblocking and opening up the pores, not, however, by any drawing power. The former, then, are of the same kind as purgatives, the latter as those which generate milk and seed, about which we shall speak a little later, after we have first gone through the cough-drugs, since these too are named in a twofold way, some from producing coughs, others only from stopping them. Their powers are most opposite to one another. For some are of a thinning nature, others of a thickening one. The thinning power, then, arises in hot and fine-particled substances, the thickening in cold and coarse-particled ones. Let this discussion, then, end here. It is fitting to pass on to the one deferred a little earlier.

Greek (Kühn)

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: βηχικὰ
For there is a certain community and likeness of actions and powers both 771 in the things that promote and in those that stop menses, milk and seed. And somehow adjacent to these lie the diuretics and the cough-remedies (βηχικά), wherefore one must speak about each of them in turn. The powers productive of milk and seed are some of them in drugs only, others also in foods. In drugs, whenever by thoroughly heating the phlegmatic humours we change them into blood; in foods, according to the likeness of the whole substance, and whenever they are of good humour and moderately moist and of a lukewarm heat, of just such a kind as milk also is. For the blood partakes of a heat commensurate for the animal, while the yellow bile partakes of a heat greater than the measure, just as phlegm partakes of coldness. Milk, so far as concerns heat, stands in the middle between phlegm and blood, yet it does not stand at an equal distance from each, but is farther from phlegm and nearer to blood. So whenever milk is collected insufficiently in the breasts and you wish more to be produced, examine the matter of the blood: for either it is less than is fitting or more faulty.

For there is a certain community and likeness of actions and powers among 771 the things that provoke and that stop menses, milk and semen. Lying somehow alongside them are the diuretics and the cough remedies, so each of these must also be discussed in turn. Whenever, then, milk collects deficiently in the breasts and you wish more of it to be produced, examine the blood; for it is either less than is proper or more depraved.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
The 772 lesser, then, requires that the whole regimen be moistening and warming; the more faulty, if it is bilious, requires first purgation, and then the kind of regimen that has been described; but if it is phlegmatic, it requires drugs that warm, either in the first or in the second order, yet do not dry. Better among these are those which are not only drugs but also foods: rocket and fennel and dill. And I mean the herbs themselves while still green and moist; for when dry they both dry and warm more than is needed. Of this kind too are smyrnion and celery and sion and polion, these too green, before they have been dried. For indeed all drying things, feeding upon the moisture of the blood, render it at once thicker and less, and hotter than it should be, if they are hot by nature, but colder, if cold.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: σίον, ἰκμάδα, τάξιν
The 772 deficient blood, then, requires the whole regimen to be moistening and warming; blood of worse quality, if it is bilious, requires first purging and then a regimen of the kind described; if it is phlegmatic, it requires drugs that warm, either in the first or in the second order, but that do not dry. Better than these are those that are not drugs only but also foods: rocket, fennel and dill. I mean the herbs themselves while they are still green and moist, for when dry they both dry and warm more than is needed. To this class belong also smyrnium, celery, sion and polium, these too green, before they have been dried. For all drying things, consuming the moisture of the blood, render it at once thicker and less in quantity, and hotter than it should be if they are hot in nature, colder if they are cold.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And surely it needs to be moderately hot and in no way thick for the generation of milk; whence all things of that sort extinguish milk rather than generate it, whereas those that heat, as has been said, but share in no evident dryness, were reasonably said to have 773 powers generative of milk. These, then, are few in number; for it is not at all easy to hit upon the aforesaid due proportion in the mixture. But infinite, as one might so put it, and beyond enumeration are those that harm milk; for both those that heat more than they should, and those that dry, and those that cool—the former damaging the quality of the blood, the latter making its whole substance less—prevent milk from coming to be. Similar in power to those just mentioned are those things which are naturally such as to provoke or to stop the menses. For the matter common to both is the blood in the veins. This, then, whenever it is at once free-flowing and moderate in quantity, furnishes an abundant supply to each of the two. And indeed, that there are not small veins common to the breasts and the womb, and which these happen to be, has been stated in other works. For this reason, whenever the blood is carried to the one of the two parts, the other is dried up.

The blood, I suppose, needs to be moderately hot and in no way thick for the generation of milk. Hence all things of this kind quench milk rather than generate it, whereas those that heat, as has been said, but have no share in any evident dryness, have reasonably been said to possess powers generative of milk 773. But the things that harm milk are, as one might put it, countless and beyond enumeration. Those that heat more than is needed, those that dry, and those that cool all prevent milk from being generated: some by damaging the quality of the blood, others by making its whole substance less. A power closely similar to those just mentioned belongs to the things that by nature promote or stop the menses. For the material common to both is the blood in the veins. When this is at once free-flowing and moderate in quantity, it furnishes an abundant supply to each. It has, moreover, been stated elsewhere that there are veins, and not small ones, common to the breasts and the uterus, and which these happen to be. For this reason, whenever the blood is carried to one of the two sets of parts, the other is dried up.

And for this reason, those women in whom the menses flow in due measure do not collect milk in the breasts, and those in whom 774 this is abundant have their menses completely held back. It is therefore no wonder if the regimens and the drugs that generate and those that stop the purgation from the womb in each month and the generation of milk in the breasts are similar. But those [purgations] that have been impaired to a greater degree, or are even entirely held back, none of such things any longer cures; rather savin 775 and meum, iris and calamint, pennyroyal and dittany, asarum and costus, cassia and cinnamon, amomum and aristolochia, and bounion, and whatever others are of this kind, cure the complete suppressions of the purgations of the womb. Ὥσπερ δὲ γάλακτος, οὕτω καὶ σπέρματος εἰώθασιν ὀνομάζειν ἔνια μὲν γεννητικὰ, καὶ τούτοις ἐναντία τὰ σβεστικὰ, προκλητικὰ δ’ ἕτερα, καὶ τούτοις ἐναντία τὰ ἐπισχετικά. γεννητικὰ μὲν οὖν ἐστι τὰ γεννῶντα τὸ μὴ πρότερον ὑπάρχον, σβεστικὰ δὲ τὰ φθείροντα· προκλητικὰ δὲ τὰ τὸ συνηθροισμένον ἐν βάθει πρὸς τοὐμφανὲς ἄγοντα, καὶ τούτοις ἐναντία τὰ ἐπισχετικά. γεννητικὰ μὲν οὖν σπέρματός ἐστιν ἐδέσματα ὅσα τρόφιμά τε ἅμα καὶ φυσώδη καὶ ταῖς ὅλαις οὐσίαις ἐστὶν οἰκεῖα· φάρμακα δ’ ὅσα πνευματώδη καὶ θερμά· σβεστικὰ δὲ τὰ ξηραίνοντα πάντα καὶ τὰ ψύχοντα πάντα καὶ ταῖς οὐσίαις ἐναντία, καὶ μὲν δὴ καὶ προκλητικὰ μὲν ὅσα φυσώδη τ’ ἐστὶ καὶ θερμὰ χωρὶς τοῦ ξηραίνειν, ἐπισχετικὰ δὲ τὰ ἐναντία.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πνευματώδη, ταῖς ὅλαις οὐσίαις
And for this reason such drugs have been called diuretic par excellence, not because they alone set urination in motion, but because they set it in motion alone, without menses and 776 milk. For, in addition to everything else, the drying drugs are not even suitable for expectorations from the chest and lung. For those too need to be cut, but not dried, just as indeed the stones in the kidneys and bladder do. But such drugs have been discussed earlier. Just as in the case of milk, so too in the case of seed, people are accustomed to name some drugs generative, and those opposed to these quenching, others provocative, and those opposed to these checking. Generative, then, are those that generate what did not previously exist, and quenching those that destroy it; provocative are those that bring what has been gathered in the depth to the surface, and those opposed to these are the checking ones. Generative of seed, then, are those foods that are at once nourishing and flatulent and akin to the whole substances; and those drugs that are pneumatic and hot; quenching are all those that dry and all those that cool and are opposed to the substances; and furthermore provocative are those that are flatulent and hot without drying, while checking are their opposites.

And for this reason such drugs have been called diuretic in a special sense: not because they alone stimulate urination, but because they stimulate urination alone, without menses and 776 milk. For, besides the other points, drying drugs are not suitable for expectorations from the thorax and lung either. Those too need to be cut but not dried, just as the stones in the kidneys and bladder do. Such drugs have been discussed earlier. Just as with milk, so too with semen, they are accustomed to call some drugs generative, and the ones opposite to these extinguishing; others provocative, and the ones opposite to these checking. Generative, then, are those that generate what did not exist before, and extinguishing those that destroy it. Provocative are those that bring what has been collected in the depth to the surface, and the ones opposite to these are checking. Generative of semen, then, among foods are those that are at once nourishing and flatulent and akin in their whole substances; among drugs, those that are pneumatic and hot. Extinguishing are all drying ones, all cooling ones, and those contrary in their substances. And moreover provocative are those that are flatulent and hot without drying, and checking are the opposite ones.

τῆς γὰρ τοῦ σπέρματος 777 οὐσίας ἐκ χρηστοῦ περιττώματος ἐχούσης τὴν γένεσιν, οὔσης δὲ καὶ πνευματώδους, τρόφιμά τε ἅμα καὶ πνευματώδη χρὴ πάνθ’ ὑπάρχειν ὅσα γεννᾷν καὶ προκαλεῖσθαι δύνηται σπέρμα. βολβοὶ μὲν οὖν ἐρέβινθοί τε καὶ κύαμοι καὶ πολύποδες καὶ κῶνος ἐδέσματα πολύσπερμα λέγεται καὶ ἔστι· σκίγκος δὲ καὶ σατύριον φάρμακα. τροφαὶ δ’ ἅμα καὶ φάρμακα πολύσπερμα τό τε τῆς λίνου σπέρμα καὶ τὸ εὔζωμον. ὅσα δὲ ψύχειν πέφυκεν ἐδέσματά τε καὶ φάρμακα, παχύνοντά τε καὶ πηγνύντα, καὶ στάσιμον ἐργαζόμενα τὸ σπέρμα, τὴν τῶν ἐπισχόντων αὐτὰ δύναμιν, οὐ τὴν τῶν φθειρόντων ἔχει. θριδακίναι μὲν γὰρ καὶ βλίτα καὶ ἀτραφάξυες καὶ κολοκυνθὶς καὶ μόρα καὶ μηλοπέπονές τε καὶ σίκυοι, ἤν τε καὶ πέπονες, ἤν τε καὶ μὴ πέπονες ὦσιν. ὅσα δὲ ξηραίνει, τὴν ἀρχὴν οὐδ’ ἐπιτρέπει γενέσθαι τὸ σπέρμα, κᾂν θερμὰ τὴν φύσιν ὑπάρχῃ, καθάπερ τὸ πήγανον. εἰ δὲ καὶ μὴ θερμαίνει, πολὺ μᾶλλον, ὡς ἡ νυμφαία. ταῦτα μέν γε καὶ κατὰ τὴν ἰδιότητα τῆς οὐσίας εὔλογον ἐναντίως ἔχειν σπέρματι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κῶνος, κολοκυνθὶς, μηλοπέπονές
For since the substance of the seed 777 has its generation from a useful residue, and is also pneumatic, all those things that are able to generate and to provoke seed must be at once nutritive and pneumatic. Bulbs, then, and chickpeas and beans and octopuses and pine-nut are called, and are, foods rich in seed; skink and satyrion are drugs. Nourishments and at the same time drugs rich in seed are the seed of flax and rocket. But all those foods and drugs whose nature is to cool, which thicken and congeal and render the seed stagnant, have the power of things that restrain it, not of things that destroy it. For lettuces and blites and oraches and gourd and mulberries and melons and cucumbers, whether they be ripe or whether they be not ripe. But those which dry do not permit the seed to come into being at all, even if they are hot in nature, like rue; and if they do not heat either, much more so, like the water-lily. These, at any rate, it is reasonable to suppose are contrary to seed also in respect of the specific property of their substance.

For since the substance of the semen 777 has its generation from a useful residue, and is moreover pneumatic, all things that are able to generate and to call forth semen must be at once nourishing and pneumatic. Now bulbs, chickpeas, broad beans, octopuses and pine-cone are said to be, and are, foods productive of much semen; skink and satyrion are drugs that do so. Things that are at once nourishments and drugs productive of much semen are the seed of flax and rocket. But all foods and drugs that are by nature cooling, and that thicken and congeal the semen and make it stationary, have the power of those that check it, not of those that destroy it. Such are lettuces, blite, oraches, gourd, mulberries, melons, and cucumbers, whether they are ripe or not ripe. Those things that dry do not even allow the semen to be generated in the first place, even if they are hot in nature, as rue is. And if they do not heat as well, they do so much more, as the water-lily does. These, at any rate, may reasonably be held to be opposed to semen also through the peculiar property of their substance.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
And the same analogy of foods and 778 drugs can be found in the case of milk and of the menses. In another way, there fall under the same genus as these all those things that assist the expectorations from the respiratory organs and all those that are naturally such as to move urination. For all these are of thinning power, just as their opposites are of thickening power. But concerning the thinning diet a separate work has been written, in which, in the same book, the thickening things are no less made clear. As for the thinning and thickening drugs, select them from the three books that follow these, taking as your aim, in the case of the thinning ones, those that have heat and fineness of parts, and in the case of the thickening ones, the opposites. For it has been shown by us in what precedes that the nature of fine-particled drugs is a distinct one, belonging not only to those that heat; and in the same way also that of coarse-particled ones belongs not only to those that cool. And just as in the case of such things there is a certain analogy in their powers, in the same way one must seek it in the case of all, even if no established name happens to exist.

The same analogy of foods and 778 drugs is also to be found in the case of milk and of the menses. In another way, there fall under the same genus with them those things that assist expectorations from the respiratory organs and those that naturally provoke urination. For all of these are of thinning power, just as their opposites are of thickening power. But on the thinning diet I have written separately, and in the same book the thickening things are set out no less. The thinning and thickening drugs, however, you should select from the three books that follow these, taking as your mark, for the thinning ones, those that have heat and fine-particledness, and, for the thickening ones, the opposite. For we have shown in the foregoing that the nature of fine-particled drugs is a distinct one, belonging not only to heating drugs; and in the same way the nature of coarse-particled drugs belongs not only to cooling ones. And just as in such cases there exists a certain analogy among the powers, in the same way one should search in all cases, even if there happens to be no established name.

λέγονται γοῦν τινες ὀξυδορκικαὶ δυνάμεις αἱ τοὺς ὀφθαλμοὺς, ἐπειδὰν μηδὲν ﻿ 779 ἔχοντες αἰσθητὸν πάθημα φαύλως ἐνεργῶσιν, ἐπανορθούμεναι. καὶ χρὴ δηλονότι καὶ κατὰ τὰ ὦτα καὶ τὰς ῥῖνας ἑκάστην τε τῶν ἄλλων αἰσθήσεων ἀνάλογον ὑπάρχειν τινὰ τοιαύτην δύναμιν, οὐ μὴν ὠνόμασταί γε ὀξυήκοός τις δύναμις, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ κατὰ τὴν ὄσφρησιν ἢ τὴν γεῦσιν ἢ τὴν ἁφὴν, καίτοι γε ἐν αὐτοῖς τοῖς αἰσθητοῖς πράγμασιν ἀναλόγως ὑπαρχούσης. εἰ δ’ ἐπὶ πλέον ἐκτείνοι τις τὴν ὁμοιότητα, καὶ καθ’ ἕκαστον ἡμῶν μόριον ἀναγκαῖον εἶναι τοιαύτην τινὰ δύναμιν φαρμάκων, ὑφ’ ἧς δηλονότι βέλτιον γίγνεται τὸ τοῦ μέρους ἔργον, ὥσπερ, εἰ οὕτως ἔτυχε, τὸ τῆς γαστρός. ἔστι γὰρ οὖν κᾀνταῦθα πολλάκις εὑρεῖν ἀτονίαν τινὰ τοιαύτην αὐτῆς, οἵα κατ’ ὀφθαλμούς ἐστιν ἡ ἀμβλυωπία, μήτε φλεγμαίνοντας μήτε ῥευματιζομένους μήθ’ ἡλκωμένους μήτ’ ἄλλο τι φανερὸν ἔχοντας κακόν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
At any rate, certain powers are called 'sharp-sighting' (oxydorkic), namely those which set the eyes right when, though having no perceptible affection, they function badly. 779 And clearly there must also exist, in the case of the ears and the nostrils and each of the other senses, analogously, some such power; yet no 'sharp-hearing' power has in fact received a name, just as none has in the case of smell or taste or touch, even though in the perceptible things themselves it exists analogously. And if one were to extend the similarity still further, there would necessarily be, in the case of each of our parts as well, some such power of drugs, by which, evidently, the function of the part becomes better—as, for instance, that of the stomach. For here too one can often find a certain atony of it of such a kind as dim-sightedness is in the case of the eyes, when they are neither inflamed nor afflicted with flux nor ulcerated nor have any other manifest evil.

At any rate, certain powers are called 'oxydorcic' (sight-sharpening): those which correct the eyes when, having no 779 perceptible affection, they nonetheless function poorly. And clearly there must also be some such power, analogously, for the ears and the nostrils and for each of the other senses. Yet no 'sharp-hearing' power has been given a name, just as none has for the sense of smell or taste or touch, even though such a power exists analogously in the very matters of sense themselves. And if one were to extend the similarity further, there must also be, for each of our parts, some such power of drugs, by which, evidently, the function of the part becomes better: as, for example, the function of the stomach. For here too one can often find a certain atony of it, of the same kind as dimness of sight is in the eyes, when they are neither inflamed nor suffering a flux nor ulcerated nor have any other evident ill.
