Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κνίκῳ, λιβανίῳ
And indeed the drugs that correct such a condition of the stomach have been called peptic (digestive), not having, in the form of their appellation, anything 780 analogous to the sharp-sight-producing drugs, but being closely similar in their action and power. Since we also said that certain powers have been named from parts of the body, it would be no bad thing to go through these too. For they are suited neither, as one might suppose, to all the affections of the part, nor to some one affection in such a way as to benefit no other; rather, they have received their appellation from the part either because they are used most often, or because they act most effectively, upon this particular part. Ophthalmic powers, because we use them both most effectively and most often upon the eyes, as for instance the collyrium of roses and that of pompholyx and the one called knikos and the libanium and all the others, which one may also use when the ears are affected, and the mouth, and the nostrils, and the genitals and the seat, yet neither so often nor so beneficially. And again hepatic, splenic, pleuritic and stomachic powers, since we see these too benefiting each of the parts mentioned both most effectively and most often.

And indeed the drugs that correct such a condition of the stomach have been called 'digestive' (πεπτικά). In the form of their designation they are not 780 analogous to the 'sharp-sighting' ones, but in their effect and in their power they are closely similar. Since we said that some powers have also been named from parts of the body, it would not be amiss to go through these as well. For they are neither suited, as one might suppose, to all the affections of the part, nor to a single one in such a way as to benefit nothing else. Rather, they got their designation from the part either because [they are used for it] most often, or because they act most of all upon that particular part. They are called ophthalmic because we use them both most of all and most often on the eyes. Such are the collyrium made from roses, the one made from pompholyx, the one called Cnicus, the Libanium, and all the others. It is possible to use these also when the ears are affected, and the mouth, the nose, the genitals and the seat, though neither so often nor so beneficially. Again, [we call powers] hepatic, splenic, pleuritic and stomachic because we see these, too, benefiting each of those mentioned most of all and most often.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: καινότητας
But what the cause of the benefit 781 is will be stated in the treatise on the Therapeutic Method, in which we shall also go through all the other matters concerning the use of drugs, so that there is no need to prolong things further here, except perhaps to remark briefly on the use of names which certain other physicians among the more recent ones introduced in their innovating, and not least those called Methodics. For that certain powers are said by them to be checking, or constricting, or contracting, or repelling, or something else of that sort, is neither absurd nor obscure. But when they say that certain materials or powers are metasyncritic, what is said is neither clear nor true nor proper to their sect, seeing that they claim to shun the indication derived from dogmatic suppositions, since the manifest communities (καινότητας) suffice for them. But that, when the pores of the body are turned aside from the state according to nature into the state contrary to nature, certain affections are constituted in the peculiar character of their alteration, is a dogmatic supposition.

What the cause of the benefit is 781 will be stated in the treatise on the Therapeutic Method, in which we also go through everything else concerning the use of drugs, so that there is no need to lengthen the discussion here any further, except perhaps to remark briefly on the use of names which certain others of the more recent physicians introduced as innovators, and not least the so-called Methodists. For that they speak of certain 'checking' (stalticai) powers, that is, constricting, or drawing together, or repelling, or something else of that kind, is neither absurd nor obscure. But when they say that certain materials or powers are 'metasyncritic', what is said is neither clear nor true nor proper to their sect, since they claim to avoid the indication taken from dogmatic suppositions, for, they say, the apparent novelties suffice them. But that, when the pores of the body are turned aside from the natural state into the unnatural, certain affections arise according to the particular character of their alteration, is a dogmatic supposition.

Greek (Kühn)
ἕπεται δὲ ταύτῃ καὶ τὸ τῶν τοιούτων παθῶν 782 μετασυγκριτικάς τινας εἶναι δυνάμεις, ἀλλοιούσας δηλονότι τὴν παρὰ φύσιν τῆς συγκρίσεως ἡμῶν ποροποιίαν. οὕτως γὰρ δὴ καὶ χρῶνται τοῖς ὀνόμασιν οἱ περὶ τὸν Θεσσαλὸν, εἶτ’ ἐπὶ τῶν χρονίως ῥευματιζομένων παραλαμβάνουσι τὰς τοιούτοις δυνάμεις, οὔθ’ ὅτι τῶν πόρων ἀλλοίωσις ἐν τοῖς τοιούτοις γίνεται πάθεσιν ἀποδείξαντες οὔθ’ ὅτι τάδε τὰ φάρμακα μετασυγκρίνειν πέφυκεν, τὴν ἡμαρτημένην ποροποιίαν ἐπιδεῖξαι δυνάμενοι. διὰ τί γὰρ, ὦ πρὸς θεῶν, τὸ νᾶπυ καὶ ἡ θαψία καὶ τὸ πύρεθρον, ἥ τε τῆς καππάρεως ῥίζα, καὶ ἁπλῶς ὅσα καίειν πέφυκεν, εἰ χρονίσειεν ὁμιλοῦντα τῷ σώματι, μετασυγκρίνει τὴν ποροποιίαν; ὅτι μὲν γὰρ ὠφελεῖται τὰ ῥευματικὰ μόρια πρὸς τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων ἐναργῶς φαίνεται. διὰ τί δ’ ὠφελεῖται, πρόβλημά ἐστι τῆς φυσικῆς θεωρίας. ἡμεῖς μὲν οὖν φαμεν ὑγρὰν καὶ ψυχρὰν εἶναι τὴν κρᾶσιν αὐτῶν, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο δεῖσθαι τῶν θερμαινόντων τε καὶ ξηραινόντων. ἄλλοι δ’ ἄλλην τὴν αἰτίαν ἐροῦσιν, ὡς ἂν καὶ τύχωσι περὶ τῶν στοιχείων τοῦ σώματος ὑπολαμβάνοντες.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τοιούτοις
And it follows upon this also that there are, for such affections, 782 certain metasyncritic powers, namely ones that alter the pore-formation of our composite that is contrary to nature. For this is indeed how Thessalus and his followers use the names, and then in the case of those who are chronically afflicted with fluxes they employ such powers, having neither demonstrated that an alteration of the pores occurs in such affections nor being able to show that these particular drugs are by nature such as to metasyncrise the faulty pore-formation. For why, in the gods' name, do mustard and thapsia and pellitory, and the root of the caper, and in general all things whose nature is to burn, if they remain for a long time in contact with the body, metasyncrise the pore-formation? For that the rheumatic parts are benefited by such drugs is clearly evident; but why they are benefited is a problem of natural theory. We, then, say that their mixture is moist and cold, and that for this reason they need things that heat and dry; but others will state a different cause, according as they happen to hold views about the elements of the body.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τοιούτοις (with δυνάμεις; probably corrupt for τοιαύτας, translated as 'such'), ἐπιδεῖξαι δυνάμενοι (the sense seems to require a negative, 'nor being able to point out'; the text may be defective), τῆς συγκρίσεως ἡμῶν (rendered 'our compound', Methodist/Asclepiadean technical sense)
And from this assumption there follows also the claim that for such affections 782 there are certain metasyncritic powers, namely powers that alter the unnatural pore-formation of our compound. For it is precisely in this way that Thessalus and his followers use these terms, and then they apply such powers to those who suffer chronically from flux, without having demonstrated either that an alteration of the pores occurs in such affections or that these drugs are by nature such as to effect metasyncrisis, nor being able to point out the faulty pore-formation. For why, in the name of the gods, do mustard, thapsia and pellitory, and the root of the caper, and in short all things whose nature is to burn, if they remain in contact with the body for a long time, effect a metasyncrisis of the pore-formation? That the fluxional parts are benefited by such drugs is plainly evident; but why they are benefited is a problem for natural inquiry. We, then, say that their mixture is moist and cold, and that for this reason they need things that heat and dry; but others will give other causes, according to whatever assumptions they happen to hold about the elements of the body.

οὐ μὴν προσήκει γε φθέγγεσθαι 783 τὸ τῆς μετασυγκρίσεως ὄνομα τῶν ἀφ’ ἑτέρας αἱρέσεως ἀγομένων οὐδενὶ, πλὴν ὅσοι τὰ τῶν ζώων σώματα συγκρίματα νομίζουσί τε καὶ ὀνομάζουσιν, ἐξ ὄγκων ἀτόμων ἢ ἐλαχίστων ἢ ἀμερῶν ἢ ὁμοιομερῶν, καὶ οὐδὲ τούτοις ἅπασι, ἀλλ’ ὅσοι, καθάπερ ὁ Θεσσαλὸς, ἐν τῷ κανόνι τῆς τῶν πόρων ἀλλοιώσεως τὰ τοιαῦτα τῶν νοσημάτων ἀνατιθέασιν, οὐ γὰρ ᾗ μεθοδικός ἐστι, τοῦτο λέγων ὁ Θεσσαλός. ἀποχωρεῖ γὰρ ὅταν ὡς μεθοδικὸς ἡμῖν διαλέγηται τῆς ἀπὸ τῶν ἀδήλων ἐνδείξεως, ἀλλ’ ᾗ δογματικὸς, ἀπὸ μὲν τῶν αὐτῶν Ἀσκληπιάδει στοιχείων ἀρχόμενος, οὐ μὴν ἀκολουθῶν γε πάντῃ, ἀλλ’ οὐ νῦν γε καιρὸς ὑπὲρ τῶν τοιούτων ἐπὶ πλέον διεξέρχεσθαι. τὰ μὲν γὰρ αὐτῶν ἐστιν οἰκειότερα τῇ θεραπευτικῇ μεθόδῳ, τὰ δ’ ἐν τοῖς περὶ τῆς κατὰ Θεμίσωνα καὶ Θεσσαλὸν αἱρέσεως ἀκριβέστερον εἰρήσεται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀγομένων, ἐλαχίστων
It is not, however, fitting for anyone who is led by another sect to utter 783 the name of 'metasyncrisis', except for those who both consider and name the bodies of animals 'compounds' (synkrimata), composed of masses that are atomic, or minimal, or partless, or homoeomerous; and not even for all of these, but for those who, like Thessalus, refer such diseases to the rule of the alteration of the pores—for Thessalus does not say this insofar as he is a Methodist. For whenever he discourses with us as a Methodist, he withdraws from the indication drawn from hidden things; rather, [he says it] insofar as he is a dogmatist, beginning from the same elements as Asclepiades, though certainly not following him in every respect. But this is not the occasion to go through such matters at greater length. For some of them are more proper to the Therapeutic Method, and others will be stated more precisely in the [books] on the sect of Themison and Thessalus.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐν τῇ τῶν ἔμπροσθεν ἀναβληθέντων
It is therefore now time to bring this book too to a close here, after adding to it two further heads: the one falling among the matters deferred earlier, concerning the contrary powers; the other necessary for the book that follows, and defining the 784 order of each of the drugs that heat and cool, moisten and dry. The contrary powers, then, are to be judged from their effects, as we have shown many times throughout this whole treatise, when we said that certain powers are clogging, contrary to the cleansing ones, unblocking contrary to the obstructing ones, softening contrary to the hardening ones, and relaxing contrary to the tensing ones. For astringent, or pungent or bitter or sweet or sour, we showed to be not powers of drugs but gustatory qualities, so that either the contrariety is not to be sought in these at all, or it is not to be sought as in powers. And the point of reference even in these is twofold: their activities and their mixtures. The activities consist in condensing and rarefying, or drawing something from the depth or repelling it, and thickening the materials or thinning them, or blocking the pores or unblocking them.

It is time, then, to bring this discussion too to a close here, after adding to it two further main headings. The one belongs among the matters deferred earlier and concerns contrary powers. The other is necessary for the discussion that follows, and defines the 784 order of each of the heating and cooling, moistening and drying drugs. Contrary powers, then, must be judged from their effects, as we have often shown in this whole treatise. We said that certain clogging powers are contrary to the cleansing ones, unblocking ones to blocking ones, softening ones to hardening ones, and relaxing ones to tensing ones. For astringent, pungent, bitter, sweet or sour we showed to be not powers of drugs but gustatory qualities. So either one must not seek contrariety in these at all, or one must not seek it as one does among powers. Yet even in these the aim is twofold: their activities and their mixtures. The activities lie in condensing and rarefying; or in drawing out something from the depth, or repelling it; and in thickening the materials or thinning them; or in blocking the pores or unblocking them.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
The mixtures, on the other hand, [are to be judged] according to hot and cold, moist and dry, concerning all of which a sufficient determination has been made in the fourth book, 785 where we showed that the astringent is earthy and cold, the sour fine-particled and cold, the bitter earthy and fine-particled, and that which is cold without any notable quality watery. So too the pungent was shown to be fiery, and the salty earthy and hot, though not yet fiery. Likewise also the sweet is hot, but not yet caustic. And all oily things are both watery and airy. And their actions, surely, followed upon their mixtures. For the astringent is of such a nature as to contract, compress, condense, repel and thicken, and, before all these, to cool and dry; the sour to cut, divide and thin, to unblock and cleanse thoroughly without heating; and the pungent acts similarly to the sour, at least as regards thinning thoroughly and cleansing thoroughly. But it differs in that the sour cools while the pungent heats, and further in that the former repels while the latter draws and disperses.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πιλεῖν, ἀποκρούεσθαι, οὐ μὴν ἤδη γέ πω
The mixtures, on the other hand, concern the hot and the cold, the wet and the dry; all of these were sufficiently distinguished in the fourth book 785, where we showed that the astringent is earthy and cold, the sour fine-particled and cold, the bitter earthy and fine-particled, and that which is without any distinctive quality cold and watery. In the same way the pungent too was shown to be fiery, and the salty earthy and hot, yet not, however, already fiery. Likewise the sweet too is hot, but not yet caustic. And whatever is oily is all both watery and airy. And their works, of course, followed upon their mixtures. For the astringent is by nature such as to draw together, compress, condense, repel and thicken, and, before all these, to cool and dry; the sour to cut, divide and thin, to unblock and cleanse thoroughly without heating; and the pungent acts similarly to the sour, at least in thinning out and cleansing thoroughly. But it differs in that the sour cools while the pungent heats, and further in that the one repels, while the other draws and disperses.

Greek (Kühn)
οὕτω δὲ καὶ τὸ μὲν πικρὸν διακαθαίρει τε τοὺς πόρους καὶ διαῤῥύπτει καὶ λεπτύνει καὶ 786 τέμνει τὸ πάχος τῶν χυμῶν ἄνευ φανερᾶς θερμότητος. τὸ δ’ ὑδατῶδες ψυχρὸν παχύνει καὶ συνίστησι καὶ συνάγει καὶ πιλεῖ καὶ νεκροῖ καὶ ναρκοῖ. τὸ δὲ δριμὺ λεπτύνει, διακαθαίρει, διαφορεῖ καὶ ἐκρήσσει καὶ ἐπισπᾶται καὶ ἐσχαροῖ. τὸ δὲ ἁλυκὸν συνάγει καὶ σφίγγει, ταριχεύει, ξηραίνει χωρὶς ἐπισήμου θερμότητος ἢ ψύξεως. τὸ δὲ γλυκὺ πέττει, χαλᾷ, ἀραιοῖ. τὸ δ’ ἐλαιῶδες ὑγραίνει, μαλάττει, χαλᾷ. ῥᾴδιον οὖν ἤδη τοῖς εἰρημένοις σταθμώμενον ἐξευρίσκειν, τῶν χυμῶν ὅσοι τε ταῖς κράσεσιν καὶ ὅσοι τοῖς ἔργοις εἰσὶν ἐναντίοι. Ἐμοὶ δὲ καιρὸς ἂν εἴη πρέπων ἀφορισαμένῳ τὰς τάξεις τῶν ἐν τοῖς φαρμάκοις κράσεών τε καὶ δυνάμεων ἐνταῦθα καταπαύειν ἤδη τὸν λόγον. τὸ μὲν δὴ σύμμετρον φάρμακον, ὡς ὁμοίας εἶναι κράσεως ᾧ προσάγεται, κατὰ τὸ μήτε ξηραίνειν μήθ’ ὑγραίνειν, μήτε ψύχειν μήτε θερμαίνειν, οὔτε ξηρὸν οὔθ’ ὑγρὸν, οὔτε ψυχρὸν οὔτε θερμὸν ὀνομάζειν προσήκει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ταριχεύει, ἐκρήσσει
So too the bitter thoroughly cleanses the pores and scours and thins and 786 cuts the thickness of the humours without evident heat. The watery cold thickens and congeals and contracts and compacts and deadens and numbs. The pungent thins, thoroughly cleanses, disperses, and bursts open and draws and forms an eschar. The salty contracts and binds tight, preserves as in pickling, dries without notable heat or cooling. The sweet concocts, relaxes, rarefies. The oil-like moistens, softens, relaxes. It is therefore now easy, measuring by what has been said, to discover which of the flavours are opposite in their mixtures and which in their actions. For me it would be a fitting moment, once I have defined the orders of the mixtures and powers in drugs, to bring the discourse to a close here. Now the well-proportioned drug, such that it is of a mixture like that of the body to which it is applied, inasmuch as it neither dries nor moistens, nor cools nor heats, it is appropriate to call neither dry nor moist, nor cold nor hot.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐκρήσσει, διαῤῥύπτει, ταριχεύει, τάξεις
In the same way, the bitter cleanses the pores, scours them, thins, and 786 cuts the thickness of the humours, without evident heat. The cold watery thickens, condenses, contracts, compacts, deadens and numbs. The pungent thins, cleanses, disperses, breaks open, draws, and forms an eschar. The salty contracts and binds tight, preserves as in pickling, and dries, without distinctive heat or cooling. The sweet concocts, relaxes, rarefies. The oil-like moistens, softens, relaxes. It is now easy, judging by what has been said, to discover which of the flavours are contrary in their mixtures and which in their actions. For my part, it would be a fitting moment, once I have defined the orders of the mixtures and powers in drugs, to bring the discussion to a close here. The well-proportioned drug, being of a mixture like that of the thing to which it is applied, in that it neither dries nor moistens, neither cools nor heats, ought to be called neither dry nor moist, neither cold nor hot.

Greek (Kühn)
τὸ δ’ ἤτοι ξηρότερον ἢ ὑγρότερον, ἢ θερμότερον 787 ἢ ψυχρότερον, ἀπὸ τῆς ἐπικρατούσης καλεῖται δυνάμεως. ἀρκεῖ δὲ καθ’ ἑκάστην ἐπικράτειαν τέσσαρας ὡς πρὸς τὴν χρείαν ποιήσασθαι τάξεις, θερμὸν μὲν ὀνομάζοντας κατὰ τὴν πρώτην τάξιν ὅ τι ἂν ἡμᾶς θερμαίνῃ, μὴ μέντοι γε ἐναργῶς, ἀλλὰ μετὰ τοῦ προσδεῖσθαί τινος ἀποδείξεως λογικῆς. οὕτω καὶ ψυχρὸν καὶ ὑγρὸν καὶ ξηρὸν, ὅ τι περ ἂν ἀποδείξεως δέηται, μηδέπω τὴν ἐνέργειαν ἰσχυρὰν μηδ’ ἐναργῆ κεκτημένον. ὅσα μέντοι σαφῶς ἤδη θερμαίνειν ἢ ψύχειν ἢ ξηραίνειν ἢ ὑγραίνειν πέφυκε, δευτέρας τὰ τοιαῦτα τάξεως εἶναι λεχθήσεται. τὰ δ’ ἤδη μὲν σφοδρῶς, οὐ μὴν εἰς ἄκρον γε, τῆς τρίτης. ὅσα δ’ οὕτω θερμαίνειν πέφυκεν, ὡς ἐσχαροῦν τε καὶ καίειν, τετάρτης. οὕτω δὴ καὶ ὅσα σφοδρότερον ψύχειν πέφυκεν, ὡς ἤδη νεκροῦν, τετάρτης καὶ ταῦτα. ξηραῖνον δ’ οὐδέν ἐστιν εὑρεῖν τετάρτης τάξεως, ἄνευ τοῦ καίειν· ὅ τι γὰρ ἄκρως ξηραίνει, πάντως τοῦτο καὶ καίει.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
But that which is either drier or moister, or hotter 787 or colder, is named from the predominating power. And it suffices, under each predominance, to establish four orders with a view to practical use, calling hot in the first order whatever heats us, yet not manifestly, but with the additional need of some rational demonstration. So too cold and moist and dry, whatever requires demonstration, not yet having acquired a strong or manifest activity. Those things, however, that are already of such a nature as to heat or cool or dry or moisten clearly will be said to be of the second order. Those that do so already vehemently, yet not to the extreme, of the third. And those that are of such a nature as to heat so as both to form an eschar and to burn, of the fourth. So likewise those that are of such a nature as to cool more vehemently, so as already to mortify, these too are of the fourth. But it is not possible to find anything that dries in the fourth order without burning; for whatever dries to the extreme, this in every case also burns.
Rendering B (opus-5-5)
But that which is either drier or moister, or hotter 787 or colder, is named from the predominating power. And it suffices, for practical purposes, to establish four orders for each predominance: naming hot in the first order whatever heats us, not however manifestly, but such that some rational demonstration is additionally required. So also with cold and moist and dry: whatever needs demonstration, not yet possessing a strong or manifest activity. Those things, however, whose nature is already plainly to heat or to cool or to dry or to moisten will be said to be of the second order. Those that do so already vehemently, yet not to the extreme, of the third. And those whose nature is to heat in such a way as to produce an eschar and to burn, of the fourth. So too those whose nature is to cool more vehemently, so as already to deaden, these also are of the fourth. But it is not possible to find anything drying of the fourth order without its burning; for whatever dries to the extreme certainly also burns.

Greek (Kühn)
τῆς τρίτης μέντοι τάξεως τῶν ξηραινόντων δύναταί τι καὶ μὴ καῖον εἶναι, καθάπερ ὅσα στύφει σφοδρῶς ἅπαντα, 788 ἐξ ὧν ἐστιν ὀμφάκιον καὶ ῥοῦς καὶ στυπτηρία καὶ βαλαύστιον καὶ κικὶς ἡ ὀμφακῖτις. ἐν αὐτοῖς δὲ τούτοις τὰ μὲν ἐγγύς ἐστι τῶν καιόντων, οἷον ἥ τε λεπὶς τοῦ χαλκοῦ καὶ ὁ κεκαυμένος χαλκὸς, ὡς ἐν τῷ ἀσαφεῖ που κεῖσθαι τῆς τρίτης τε καὶ τετάρτης τάξεως. τὰ δ’ ἐν τῷ μέσῳ, καθάπερ ὁ κεκαυμένος χαλκὸς, εἰ πλυθείη, τὰ δ’ ἐν ἀρχῇ καθάπερ ὑποκυστίς. οὕτω δὲ καὶ κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν τε καὶ τετάρτην τάξιν ἔνεστι διαφορὰς ποιεῖσθαι τριττάς. οὐ γὰρ ὡσαύτως δή που τὸ καυτήριον ξηραίνει τῷ νάπυϊ, καίτοι γε ἄμφω τῆς τετάρτης τάξεώς ἐστιν. ὑπὲρ ἁπάντων δὴ τούτων ἐν τοῖς ἑξῆς διορισθήσεται καθ’ ἕκαστον ἁπλοῦν φάρμακον ἰδίᾳ.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
Of the third order of drying things, however, something can also be non-burning, as for instance all those things which are strongly astringent, 788 among which are omphacium and sumach and alum and balaustion and the unripe oak-gall (omphakitis). Among these very things, some are close to those which burn, such as scale of copper and burnt copper, so that they lie somewhere in the indistinct region between the third and the fourth order; others are in the middle, such as burnt copper if it has been washed; and others at the beginning, such as hypocistis. In the same way, in the second and the fourth order too, one can make threefold distinctions; for the cautery surely does not dry in the same way as mustard, even though both belong to the fourth order. Concerning all these matters distinctions will be drawn in what follows, under each simple drug separately.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀμφάκιον, ὁ κεκαυμένος χαλκὸς, εἰ πλυθείη, κατὰ τὴν δευτέραν τε καὶ τετάρτην τάξιν
Of the third order of drying things, however, there can be something that does not also burn, as is the case with all things that are strongly astringent, 788 among which are omphakion, sumach, alum, balaustion, and the unripe oak-gall. Among these very things, some are close to the burning ones, such as flake of copper and burnt copper, so that they lie somewhere in the indistinct region between the third and the fourth order. Others are in the middle, such as burnt copper if it has been washed, and others at the beginning, such as hypocistis. In the same way, in the second and in the fourth order too, it is possible to make threefold distinctions. For the cautery-iron surely does not dry in the same way as mustard, although both belong to the fourth order. Concerning all these things, distinctions will be drawn in what follows for each simple drug individually.
